**Georgia**[^cite_note-17] is a country in the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) region on the east coast of the [Black Sea](3386.md). It is located at the intersection of [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) and [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia)[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20] and is generally regarded as part of Europe.[^cite_note-21] It is bordered to the north and northeast by [Russia](25391.md); to the west by the Black Sea, to the south by [Turkey](11125639.md) and [Armenia](10918072.md), and to the southeast by [Azerbaijan](746.md). It has a population of 3.9 million,[^cite_note-22] of which over a third live in [Tbilisi](42298.md), the capital and largest city. [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians), who are native to the region and constitute the majority of the population, are ethno-linguistically distinct from their neighboring nations and primarily speak [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language), a [Kartvelian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartvelian_language) that has no relation to any other language family in the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDalby200238-23]&#x20;

Georgia has been inhabited since prehistory and is known for hosting the world's earliest known sites of [winemaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine).[^cite_note-bbc_2017-24][^cite_note-archeologyMethodTheory2014322-25] The [classical era](252905.md) saw the emergence of several kingdoms, such as [Colchis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colchis) and [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Iberia), that formed the nucleus of the modern Georgian state. In the early 4th century, Georgians officially [adopted Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization_of_the_Kingdom_of_Iberia), which contributed to [their unification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_the_Georgian_realm) under the [Kingdom of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Georgia). Georgia reached [its Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Golden_Age) during the [High Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Middle_Ages) under the reigns of King [David IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_IV) and his great-granddaughter [Tamar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamar_of_Georgia). Beginning in the 15th century, the kingdom [declined and disintegrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_the_Georgian_realm) under relentless attacks from hostile empires like the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire), the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), and [Persia](64213.md). Georgia requested support from the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), which ended up [gradually annexing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_within_the_Russian_Empire) the country starting in 1801.&#x20;

After the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) in 1917, Georgia briefly emerged as [an independent republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Georgia) under [German protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Poti).[^cite_note-Soviet_power-26] However, the country was [invaded and annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Georgia) by the [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) in 1921; it then became a [republic of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic). In the 1980s, an independence movement grew quickly, leading to [Georgia's secession from the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in April 1991. For much of the [subsequent decade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states), the country endured economic crises, [political instability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conflicts_in_territory_of_the_former_Soviet_Union), and secessionist wars in [Abkhazia](18933375.md) and [South Ossetia](318381.md). Following the peaceful [Rose Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Revolution) in 2003, Georgia strongly pursued a [pro-Western](21208200.md) foreign policy, introducing a series of [reforms aimed at integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potential_enlargement_of_the_European_Union) into the [European Union](9317.md) and [NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_NATO). This Western orientation led to [worsening relations with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia%E2%80%93Russia_relations), culminating in the [Russo-Georgian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War) of 2008 and continued Russian occupation of [parts of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_in_Georgia).&#x20;

Georgia is a [representative democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_democracy) governed as a [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic).[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-undp-28] It is a [developing country](78449.md) with a very high [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index) and an [emerging market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_market) economy. Sweeping economic reforms since 2003 have resulted in one of the [freest business climates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_doing_business_index) in the world, as well as greater [economic freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Freedom_of_the_World) and [transparency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Corruption_Barometer).[^cite_note-29] In 2018, Georgia became the second country [to legalize cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_in_Georgia_\(country\)), and the first former socialist state to do so. Georgia is a member of numerous international organizations, including the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe), [Eurocontrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocontrol), [BSEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_the_Black_Sea_Economic_Cooperation), [GUAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GUAM_Organization_for_Democracy_and_Economic_Development), and [Energy Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_Community). As part of the [Association Trio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_Trio), Georgia is an official candidate for [membership in the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potential_enlargement_of_the_European_Union).[^cite_note-30] Since October 2024, Georgia has been immersed in a [deep political crisis](78750834.md).&#x20;

## Etymology

### Names of Georgia

Main article: [Names of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Georgia)



"Georgia" on a medieval *mappa mundi*, AD 1320

[Ancient Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greeks) ([Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo), [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus), [Plutarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutarch), [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer), etc.) and [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) ([Titus Livius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titus_Livius), [Tacitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacitus), etc.) referred to early western Georgians as [Colchians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colchis) and eastern Georgians as [Iberians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Iberia) (*Iberoi*, Ἰβηροι in some Greek sources).[^cite_note-31]&#x20;

The first mention of the name *Georgia* is in Italian on the *[mappa mundi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mappa_mundi)* of [Pietro Vesconte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pietro_Vesconte) dated 1320.[^cite_note-32] At the early stage of its appearance in the Latin world, the name was often spelled *Jorgia*.[^cite_note-33] Lore-based theories were given by traveller [Jacques de Vitry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_de_Vitry), who explained the name's origin by the popularity of [Saint George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_George) among Georgians,[^cite_note-34] while [Jean Chardin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Chardin) thought that *Georgia* came from the Greek γεωργός ('tiller of the land').&#x20;

Modern scholarship generally derives the name from the Persian term *Gurj* ([Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language): گرج), a historical ethnonym for [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians). The ultimate origin of this term is debated, though it may be connected to *gurğān*, a region name meaning "land of wolves."[^cite_note-35] From Persian, the term was borrowed into Byzantine Greek, Slavic, and later Western European languages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze20153-36][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERapp201421-37] Its incorporation into their languages was also because of its similarity to Saint George.[^cite_note-38] The adoption of the term in [Western Europe](33800.md) coincided with increased contact during the [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades) and trade through [Black Sea](3386.md) ports controlled by [Genoese merchants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genoese_colonies). The association with Saint George, a popular figure in medieval Christendom, may have reinforced the exonym's appeal in Latin Europe.&#x20;

The native name is *Sakartvelo* ([საქართველო](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E1%83%A1%E1%83%90%E1%83%A5%E1%83%90%E1%83%A0%E1%83%97%E1%83%95%E1%83%94%E1%83%9A%E1%83%9D#Georgian); 'land of [Kartvelians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartvelians)'), derived from the [Kartli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartli) region, recorded from the 9th century and in extended usage referring to the entire medieval [Kingdom of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Georgia) prior to the 13th century. The Georgian [circumfix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumfix) *sa*-X-*o* is a standard geographic construction designating 'the area where X dwell', where X is an [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym).[^cite_note-Rapp-39] The self-designation used by ethnic Georgians is *Kartvelebi* (ქართველები, i.e. 'Kartvelians'), first attested in the *[Umm Leisun inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Leisun_inscription)* found in the [Old City of Jerusalem](302972.md), dated to the 5th or 6th century.&#x20;

The medieval *[Georgian Chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Georgian_Chronicles)* present an eponymous ancestor of the Kartvelians, [Kartlos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartlos), a great-grandson of [Japheth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japheth) whom medieval chroniclers believed to have been the root of the local name of their kingdom. However, scholars agree that the word *Kartli* is derived from the *Karts*, a proto-Kartvelian tribe that emerged as a dominant regional group in ancient times.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze20153-36] The name *Sakartvelo* (საქართველო) consists of two parts. Its root, *kartvel-i* (ქართველ-ი), specifies an inhabitant of Kartli, or Iberia as it is known in sources of the [Eastern Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Roman_Empire).[^cite_note-40]&#x20;

### State name

The official name is "Georgia" per the English-language version of the [Georgian Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Georgia_\(country\)),[^cite_note-41] adopted in 1995. In Georgia's two official languages ([Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language) and [Abkhaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abkhaz_language)), the country is named საქართველო[^cite_note-42] (*Sakartvelo*) and Қырҭтәыла (*Kərttʷʼəla*) respectively. Prior to the adoption of the constitution in 1995 and following the [dissolution of the USSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), the country was officially called the "Republic of Georgia".[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44]&#x20;

Several languages continue to use the Russian variant of the country's name, *Gruzia*, which the Georgian authorities have sought to replace through diplomatic campaigns. Since 2006, Israel,[^cite_note-45] Japan,[^cite_note-46] South Korea[^cite_note-47] and Hungary[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49] officially changed their appellation of the country to variants of the English *Georgia*.[^cite_note-50] In 2020, Lithuania became the first country in the world to adopt *Sakartvelas* in all official communications.[^cite_note-51]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Georgia_\(country\))

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Georgian (country) history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Georgian_\(country\)_history).

### Prehistory

See also: [Prehistoric Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Georgia)





*From left to right:* *[Patera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patera)* depicting Roman emperor [Marcus Aurelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_Aurelius) uncovered in Georgia, 2nd century AD. Bronze lamp featuring [Heracles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heracles) and [Ariadne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariadne), 1st century BC, Georgian National Museum

The oldest traces of [archaic humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_humans) date from approximately 1.8 million years ago in the form of the [Dmanisi hominins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmanisi_hominins), a subspecies of *[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)* representing the oldest-known fossils of hominins in [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia).[^cite_note-52] Buffered by the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains) and benefiting from the [Black Sea](3386.md) ecosystem, the region seems to have served as a [refugium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugium_\(population_biology\)) throughout the [Pleistocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleistocene),[^cite_note-53] while the first continuous primitive settlements date back to the [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic), close to 200,000 years ago.[^cite_note-54] During the [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic), settlements developed mostly in western Georgia, in the valleys of the [Rioni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rioni) and [Qvirila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qvirila) rivers.[^cite_note-55]&#x20;

Signs of agriculture date back to at least the 6th millennium BC, especially in western Georgia, while the [Mtkvari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kura_\(South_Caucasus_river\)) basin became stably populated in the 5th millennium BC, as evidenced with the rise of various cultures closely associated with the [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent), including the [Trialetian Mesolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trialetian_Mesolithic), the [Shulaveri–Shomu culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shulaveri%E2%80%93Shomu_culture), and the [Leyla-Tepe culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leyla-Tepe_culture).[^cite_note-56] Archaeological findings show that settlements in modern-day Georgia were responsible for the first use of fibers, possibly for clothing, more than 34,000 years ago.[^cite_note-57] It also hosts the earliest known [sites of winemaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_wine) (dating to c. 6000 BCE)[^cite_note-bbc_2017-24] and the [first signs of gold mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakdrisi) (3rd millennium BC).[^cite_note-58]&#x20;

The [Kura-Araxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kura%E2%80%93Araxes_culture), [Trialeti-Vanadzor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trialeti-Vanadzor_culture), and [Colchian cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colchian_culture) coincided with the development of proto-Kartvelian tribes that may have come from [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) during the expansion of the [Hittite Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittites), including the [Mushki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mushki), [Laz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laz_people), and [Byzeres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzeres). Some historians have suggested that the collapse of the Hittite world in the [Late Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Bronze_Age_collapse) led to an expansion of the influence of these tribes to the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md), notably with the [Kingdom of Tabal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabal_\(state\)).[^cite_note-59]&#x20;

### Antiquity



King [Mirian III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirian_III) converted the nation to Christianity in the 4th century.

The classical period saw the rise of several Georgian states, including [Colchis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colchis) in western Georgia, where [Greek mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology) located the [Golden Fleece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Fleece) sought after by the [Argonauts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argonauts). Archaeological evidence points to a wealthy kingdom in Colchis as early as the 14th century BC[^cite_note-60] and an extensive trade network with [Greek colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_colonisation) on the eastern Black Sea shore (such as [Dioscurias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhumi) and [Phasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phasis_\(town\))).[^cite_note-61] The region was annexed by the [Kingdom of Pontus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Pontus) and then by the [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic).&#x20;

Eastern Georgia remained a decentralized mosaic of various clans (ruled by individual *[mamasakhlisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamasakhlisi)*) until the 4th century BC when it was conquered by [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great), eventually leading to the creation of the [Kingdom of Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Iberia) under the protectorate of the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire), an early example of advanced state organization under one king and an aristocratic hierarchy.[^cite_note-62] Various wars with the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), [Parthia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire), and [Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Armenia_\(antiquity\)) made Iberia regularly change its allegiance, though it remained a Roman client state for most of its history.&#x20;

In 337, King [Mirian III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirian_III) adopted [Christianity as the state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization_of_the_Kingdom_of_Iberia) of Iberia, beginning the Christianization of the Western Caucasus region and solidly anchoring it in Rome's sphere of influence by abandoning the ancient [Georgian polytheistic religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_mythology) heavily influenced by [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism).[^cite_note-63] However, the [Peace of Acilisene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Acilisene) in 384 formalized the [Sasanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) control over the entire Caucasus, though Christian rulers of Iberia sought to rebel at times, leading to devastating wars in the 5th–6th centuries, most famously under the rule of King [Vakhtang I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vakhtang_I) who expanded Iberia to its largest historical extent by capturing all of western Georgia and building his capital at [Tbilisi](42298.md).&#x20;

### Medieval unification



Northwestern Georgia is home to the [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) [towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svan_towers) of [Ushguli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ushguli) and [Mestia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestia).

In 580, the Sasanian Empire abolished the Kingdom of Iberia, leading to the disintegration of its constituent territories into various feudal regions by the [early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages). The [Roman–Persian wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Persian_wars) plunged the region into chaos, with both [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persia) and [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) supporting various warring factions in the Caucasus, however, the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) was able to establish control over Georgian territories by the end of the 6th century, ruling Iberia indirectly through a local *[Kouropalates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kouropalates)*.[^cite_note-64]&#x20;

In 645, the Arabs invaded southeastern Georgia, starting an [extended period of Muslim domination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) in the region; this also led to the establishment of several feudal states seeking independence from each other, such as the [Emirate of Tbilisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Tbilisi) and the [Principality of Kakheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kakheti-Hereti).[^cite_note-65] Western Georgia remained mostly a Byzantine protectorate, especially following the [Lazic War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lazic_War).[^cite_note-66]&#x20;



[Gelati Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gelati_Monastery), a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md)

The lack of a central government allowed the rise of the [Bagrationi dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrationi_dynasty) in the early 9th century. Consolidating lands in the southwestern region of [Tao-Klarjeti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tao-Klarjeti), Prince [Ashot I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashot_I_of_Iberia) (813–830) used infighting between Arab governors to expand his influence to Iberia and was recognized as Presiding Prince of Iberia by both the [Abbasid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) and the Byzantine Empire. Though Ashot's descendants formed competing princely lines, [Adarnase IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adarnase_IV_of_Iberia) managed to unify most Georgian lands (except for Kakheti and Abkhazia) and was crowned [King of the Iberians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_the_Iberians) in 888, restoring the monarchy that had been abolished three centuries prior.[^cite_note-67]&#x20;

In western Georgia, the [Kingdom of Abkhazia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Abkhazia) benefited from the weakening of Byzantium in the region to unify various tribes and become one of the most powerful states of the Caucasus in the 8th century.[^cite_note-68] In the 9th and 10th centuries, Abkhazia grew its influence through several military campaigns and came to control much of Iberia and competing with the Bagrationi. Dynastic conflicts weakened Abkhazia in the second half of the 10th century while in Tao-Klarjeti, Prince [David III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_III_of_Tao) used his influence within [Byzantine Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Anatolia) to empower the Bagrationi. [Bagrat III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrat_III_of_Georgia), heir of the Bagrationi dynasty, successively became King of Abkhazia (978), Prince of Tao-Klarjeti (1000), and King of the Iberians (1008), allowing him to unify most Georgian feudal states and be crowned in 1010 as [King of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_monarchs_of_Georgia).&#x20;

14th century Georgian frescoes



Medieval monarchs of Georgia



Depiction of the Holy Trinity painted by [Damiane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damiane).

### Golden Age and division

For much of the 11th century, the nascent Georgian kingdom experienced geopolitical and internal difficulties, with various noble factions opposed to the centralization of the Georgian state. They were often backed by the Byzantine Empire, which feared a dominion of the Caucasus region by the Bagrationi dynasty, and in some instances fuelled internal conflict through aristocratic families seeking more power. However, ties between Byzantium and Georgia were normalized when the two countries faced a new common enemy, the rising [Seljuk Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire) in the 1060s. Following the decisive Byzantine defeat at the [Battle of Manzikert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manzikert) in 1071, Constantinople started to retreat from eastern Anatolia and entrusted Georgia with its administration, placing Georgia at the forefront [of war with the Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian%E2%80%93Seljuk_wars) in the 1080s.&#x20;

The Kingdom of Georgia reached its zenith in the 12th to early 13th centuries. This period during the reigns of [David IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_IV) (r. 1089–1125) and his great-granddaughter [Tamar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamar_of_Georgia) (r. 1184–1213) has been widely termed as the [Georgian Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Golden_Age).[^cite_note-69] This early Georgian renaissance, which preceded its Western European analog, was characterized by impressive military victories, territorial expansion, and a cultural renaissance in architecture, literature, philosophy and the sciences.[^cite_note-70] The Golden Age of Georgia left a legacy of great cathedrals, romantic poetry and literature, and the epic poem *[The Knight in the Panther's Skin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Knight_in_the_Panther%27s_Skin)*, considered a national epic.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72]&#x20;



[Queen Tamar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamar_of_Georgia), the first woman to rule Georgia.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEastmond2010109-73]

David IV suppressed dissent of feudal lords and centralized power in his hands to effectively deal with foreign threats. In 1121, he decisively defeated much larger Turkish armies during the [Battle of Didgori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Didgori) and abolished the [Emirate of Tbilisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Tbilisi).[^cite_note-javaxisvili-74] The 29-year reign of Tamar, the first female ruler of Georgia, is considered the most successful in Georgian history.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEastmond201093-75] Tamar was given the title "king of kings"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEastmond2010109-73] and succeeded in neutralizing her opposition while embarking on an energetic foreign policy aided by the downfall of the rival powers of the Seljuks and Byzantium. Supported by a powerful military elite, Tamar was able to build on the successes of her predecessors to consolidate an empire that dominated the Caucasus, and extended over large parts of present-day Azerbaijan, Armenia, eastern Turkey, and northern Iran, and used the vacuum of power left by the [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade) to create the [Empire of Trebizond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Trebizond) as a Georgian vassal state.[^cite_note-76]&#x20;

The revival of the Kingdom of Georgia was set back after Tbilisi was captured and destroyed by the Khwarezmian leader [Jalal ad-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalal_al-Din_Mangburni) in 1226,[^cite_note-77] followed by devastating invasions by Mongol ruler [Genghis Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghis_Khan). The Mongols were expelled by [George V the Brilliant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_V_of_Georgia) (r. 1299–1302), known for reuniting eastern and western Georgia and restoring the country's previous strength and Christian culture. After his death, local rulers fought for their independence from central Georgian rule, until the total disintegration of the kingdom in the 15th century. Georgia was further weakened by several disastrous invasions by Mongols under [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur). Invasions continued, giving the kingdom no time for restoration, with both [Qara Qoyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara_Qoyunlu) and [Aq Qoyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Qoyunlu) Turkomans constantly raiding its southern provinces.&#x20;

### Tripartite division



King [George II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_II_of_Imereti) faced [feudal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) warfare and Ottoman invasions.

The Kingdom of Georgia [collapsed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_the_Georgian_realm) into anarchy by 1466 and fragmented into three independent kingdoms and five semi-independent [principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality). Neighbouring large empires subsequently exploited the internal division of the weakened country, and beginning in the 16th century, various Ottoman and Iranian forces subjugated western and eastern regions of Georgia, respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMikaberidze2015xxxi-78] This pushed local Georgian rulers to seek closer ties with Russia. In 1649, the [Kingdom of Imereti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Imereti) sent ambassadors to the Russian royal court, with Russia returning the favour in 1651. In the presence of these ambassadors, [Alexander III of Imereti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_III_of_Imereti) swore an oath of allegiance to Tsar [Alexis of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_of_Russia) on behalf of Imereti.[^cite_note-79] Subsequent rulers also sought assistance from Pope [Innocent XII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innocent_XII) but without success.[^cite_note-80]&#x20;

The rulers of regions that remained partly [autonomous](191145.md) organized rebellions on various occasions. As a result of incessant [Ottoman–Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Persian_Wars) and deportations, the population of Georgia dwindled to 784,700 inhabitants at the end of the 18th century.[^cite_note-81] [Eastern Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Georgia_\(country\)), composed of the regions of [Kartli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartli) and [Kakheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakheti), had been under Iranian [suzerainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzerainty) since the [Peace of Amasya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Amasya) signed with neighbouring rivalling Ottoman Turkey ([Safavid Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Georgia)). With the death of [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah) in 1747, both kingdoms broke free and were reunified through [a personal union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kartli-Kakheti) under the energetic king [Heraclius II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heraclius_II_of_Georgia), who succeeded in stabilizing Eastern Georgia to a degree.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFisherAveryHamblyMelville1991328-82]&#x20;

In 1783, Russia and the eastern Georgian [Kingdom of Kartli-Kakheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kartli-Kakheti) signed the [Treaty of Georgievsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Georgievsk), which made eastern Georgia a protectorate of Russia, guaranteed its territorial integrity and the continuation of its reigning Bagrationi dynasty in return for prerogatives in the conduct of Georgian foreign affairs.[^cite_note-83]&#x20;

Despite its commitment to defend Georgia, Russia rendered no assistance when the [Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) invaded in 1795, [capturing and sacking Tbilisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Krtsanisi) and massacring its inhabitants.[^cite_note-84] Although Russia initiated a [punitive campaign against Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Expedition_of_1796) in 1796, the Russian Imperial authorities subsequently violated key promises of the Georgievsk Treaty and in 1801 proceeded to annex eastern Georgia, while abolishing the Georgian royal [Bagrationi dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrationi_dynasty), as well as the [autocephaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephaly) of the [Georgian Orthodox Church](433340.md). [Pyotr Bagration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyotr_Bagration), one of the descendants of the abolished house of Bagrationi, later joined the Russian army and became a prominent general in the Napoleonic wars.[^cite_note-85]&#x20;

### Within the Russian Empire

Main article: [Georgia within the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_within_the_Russian_Empire)

On 22 December 1800, [Tsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar) [Paul I of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_I_of_Russia), at the alleged request of the Georgian King [George XII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgi_XII_of_Kartli-Kakheti), signed the proclamation on the incorporation of Georgia (Kartli-Kakheti) within the Russian Empire, which was finalized by a decree on 8 January 1801,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGvosdev200085-86][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAvalov1906186-87] and confirmed by Tsar [Alexander I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Russia) on 12 September 1801.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGvosdev200086-88][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELang1957249-89] The [Bagrationi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrationi) royal family was deported from the kingdom. The Georgian envoy in [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md) reacted with a note of protest that was presented to the Russian vice-chancellor, Prince Kurakin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELang1957251-90]&#x20;



During the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), numerous Georgians, like [Ivane Andronikashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivane_Andronikashvili), rose high in the imperial army ranks.

In May 1801, under the oversight of General Carl Heinrich von Knorring, Imperial Russia transferred power in eastern Georgia to the government headed by General [Ivan Petrovich Lazarev](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ivan_Petrovich_Lazarev\&action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELang1957247-91] The Georgian nobility did not accept the decree until 12 April 1802, when Knorring assembled the nobility at the [Sioni Cathedral](5304004.md) and forced them to take an oath on the [Imperial Crown of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Crown_of_Russia). Those who disagreed were temporarily arrested.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELang1957252-92]&#x20;

In the summer of 1805, Russian troops on the Askerani River near Zagam defeated the Iranian army during the [1804–13 Russo-Persian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_War_\(1804%E2%80%9313\)) and saved Tbilisi from reconquest now that it was officially part of the Imperial territories. Russian suzerainty over eastern Georgia was officially finalized with Iran in 1813 following the [Treaty of Gulistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Gulistan).[^cite_note-93] Following the annexation of eastern Georgia, the western Georgian [kingdom of Imereti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Imereti) was annexed by Tsar Alexander I. The last Imeretian king and the last Georgian Bagrationi ruler, [Solomon II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_II_of_Imereti), died in exile in 1815, after attempts to rally people against Russia and to enlist foreign support against the latter, had been in vain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESuny199464-94]&#x20;

During the [Russo-Turkish Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_Wars), numerous Georgians commanded Russian armies against the Turks, including [Ivane Andronikashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivane_Andronikashvili), [Ivane Jambakurian-Orbeliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivane_Jambakurian-Orbeliani), and [Ivane Bagration of Mukhrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivane_Bagration_of_Mukhrani). As result of these military campaigns against the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), from 1803 to 1878 several of Georgia's historic territories previously lost to the Turks – such as [Adjara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjara) – were recovered and also incorporated into the Russian Empire. The principality of [Guria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guria) was abolished and incorporated in 1829, while [Svaneti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svaneti) was gradually annexed in 1858. [Mingrelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samegrelo), although a Russian protectorate since 1803, was not absorbed until 1867.[^cite_note-95]&#x20;

Russian rule offered the Georgians security from external threats, but it was also often heavy-handed and insensitive. By the late 19th century, [discontent with the Russian authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svaneti_uprising_of_1875%E2%80%931876) grew into a national revival movement led by [Ilia Chavchavadze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilia_Chavchavadze). This period also brought social and economic change to Georgia, with new social classes emerging: the emancipation of the [serfs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serfs) freed many peasants but did little to alleviate their poverty; the growth of [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism) created an urban working class in Georgia. Both peasants and workers found expression for their discontent through revolts and strikes, culminating in the [Revolution of 1905](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_1905). The following years were marked by episodes of instability, with notable events like the [1906 Dusheti treasury heist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusheti_treasury_heist) conducted by Georgian nationalists and the subsequent [1907 Tiflis bank robbery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1907_Tiflis_bank_robbery) organized by the Bolsheviks.&#x20;

### Declaration of independence

Main article: [Democratic Republic of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Georgia)



[British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) troops marching in Batumi, Georgia, in 1920. Following [World War I](4764461.md), Britain replaced [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) troops in Georgia.

After the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) of 1917, the [Transcaucasian Democratic Federative Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcaucasian_Democratic_Federative_Republic) was established with [Nikolay Chkheidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Chkheidze) acting as its president. This weak and short-lived federation also included neighbouring Armenia and Azerbaijan.[^cite_note-Soviet_power-26] However, Georgians were not content with this cohabitation, and because of irreconcilable foreign policy differences Georgia declared independence on 26 May 1918, [becoming an ally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Poti) of the [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire).[^cite_note-\:0-96]&#x20;

Most Georgians were not supportive of Bolsheviks due to their penchant for violence and disregard for human life.[^cite_note-Rayfield2012humanlife-97] Consequently, the [Menshevik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menshevik) [Social Democratic Party of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Georgia) won the parliamentary election and its leader, [Noe Zhordania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noe_Zhordania), became prime minister, with [Nikolay Chkheidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Chkheidze) as parliamentary president. Despite the subsequent Soviet takeover, Zhordania continued to be recognized as the legitimate head of the [Georgian Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_Georgia_in_Exile) by France, UK, Belgium, and Poland through the 1930s.[^cite_note-98]&#x20;



[Samson Dadiani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samson_Dadiani), one of the drafters of the original Georgian constitution, among the world's most [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism) at the time

The 1918 [Georgian–Armenian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian%E2%80%93Armenian_War), which erupted over parts of disputed provinces between Armenia and Georgia populated mostly by Armenians, ended because of British intervention. In 1918–1919, Georgian general [Giorgi Mazniashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgi_Mazniashvili) led an [attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sochi_conflict) against the [White Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Army) led by Moiseev and Denikin to claim the [Black Sea](3386.md) coastline from [Tuapse](1623160.md) to [Sochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sochi) and [Adler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adlersky_City_District) for independent Georgia.[^cite_note-shirokov-99]&#x20;

In 1920 [Soviet Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) recognized Georgia's independence with the [Treaty of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Moscow_\(1920\)) but the Russians effectively discarded this treaty only a year later, when the Red Army [attacked Georgia in 1921](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army_invasion_of_Georgia) and formally annexed it into the Soviet Union in 1922.[^cite_note-\:0-96]&#x20;

Although short-lived, the Democratic Republic of Georgia continues to be an inspiration for contemporary Georgia due to its legacy of democracy and pluralism.[^cite_note-100] The legal and constitutional order of the first republic was considered highly progressive in its time and received international praise.[^cite_note-Lee2017-101] DRG was [one of the first countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage#Timeline) in Europe to grant women the right to vote as enshrined in the Georgian constitution, which was "unusual in most European constitutions at the time".[^cite_note-102] Several women of varying backgrounds were elected to the Georgian parliament.[^cite_note-103]&#x20;

### Soviet Socialist Republic

Main articles: [Georgian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) and [Georgian Affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Affair)

In February 1921, during the [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War), the [Red Army advanced into Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army_invasion_of_Georgia) and brought the local Bolsheviks to power. The Georgian army was defeated, and the Social Democratic government fled the country. On [25 February 1921](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Occupation_Day,_Georgia), the Red Army entered Tbilisi and established a government of workers' and peasants' [soviets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_\(council\)) with [Filipp Makharadze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipp_Makharadze) as acting head of state. Georgia was incorporated into what would soon become the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). Soviet rule was firmly established only after local insurrections were defeated.[^cite_note-104] Georgia would remain an unindustrialized periphery of the USSR until the [first five-year plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_five-year_plan_\(Soviet_Union\)) (1928–1932), when it became a major centre for textile goods.&#x20;



Georgian nationalist and anti-Soviet campaigner [Leo Kereselidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Kereselidze) was among those forced into exile by Bolshevik takeover

[Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin), an ethnic Georgian, was prominent among the Bolsheviks,[^cite_note-105] ultimately becoming the *[de facto](43594.md)* leader of the Soviet Union from 1924 until his death on 5 March 1953. Fellow Georgians such as [Lavrentiy Beria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavrentiy_Beria) and [Vsevolod Merkulov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vsevolod_Merkulov) likewise held powerful positions in the Soviet government. Stalin's [Great Purge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Purge) between 1936 and 1938 led to thousands of Georgian dissidents, intellectuals, and other presumed threats to Soviet authority being executed or sent to [Gulag penal labor camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag), severely truncating the nation's cultural and intellectual life.[^cite_note-106]&#x20;

During [World War II](32927.md), [Germany](21212.md) led an [Axis invasion of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarosa) in June 1941 with the aim of conquering all territory up to the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains). As the initial operation stalled, the Axis launched the *[Fall Blau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_Blue)* offensive in 1942 to take control of strategic Caucasian oil fields and munitions factories; ultimately, Axis troops were stopped before reaching Georgian borders. Over 700,000 Georgians—constituting about 20 percent of the population—fought in the [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) to repel the invaders and advance towards Berlin; an estimated 350,000 were killed.[^cite_note-107]&#x20;

After [Stalin's death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Joseph_Stalin), [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) became the leader of the Soviet Union and implemented a policy of [de-Stalinization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-Stalinization). Khrushchev's purges were met with [riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956_Georgian_demonstrations) in Tbilisi that had to be dispersed by military force. This violent turn of events that compromised Georgian loyalty to the Soviet Union contributed to the nation's consolidation.[^cite_note-a\&c-108] [1978 Georgian demonstrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Georgian_demonstrations) saw the return of mass anti-Soviet protests, but this time government backed down.&#x20;

Throughout the remainder of the Soviet period, Georgia's economy continued to grow and experience significant improvement, though it increasingly exhibited blatant corruption and alienation of the government from the people. With the beginning of [perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika) in 1986, the Georgian Soviet leadership proved so incapable of handling the changes that most Georgians, including [rank and file](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Special\:Search/rank_and_file) communists, concluded that the only way forward was a break from the existing Soviet system.&#x20;

### Independence, civil wars

See also: [Republic of Georgia (1990–1992)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Georgia_\(1990%E2%80%931992\))



[Georgian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_civil_war_of_1991%E2%80%931993) and the [War in Abkhazia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Abkhazia_\(1992%E2%80%931993\)) in August–October 1993

Starting in 1988, mass protests erupted in Georgia in favour of independence, led by [Georgian nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_nationalism) such as [Merab Kostava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merab_Kostava) and [Zviad Gamsakhurdia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zviad_Gamsakhurdia). The following year, the [brutal suppression by Soviet forces of a large peaceful demonstration held in Tbilisi on 4–9 April 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_9_tragedy) proved to be a pivotal event in discrediting the continuation of Soviet rule over the country.[^cite_note-109]&#x20;

In October 1990, the first multi-party [elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Georgian_Supreme_Soviet_election) were held in Soviet Georgia, which were the first multi-party elections in the entire Soviet Union, in which the opposition groups were registered as formal political parties.[^cite_note-110] The [Round Table—Free Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Round_Table%E2%80%94Free_Georgia) coalition led by Zviad Gamsakhurdia secured victory in this election and formed a new government. On 9 April 1991, shortly before the collapse of the Soviet Union, the [Supreme Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_the_Republic_of_Georgia) of Georgia [declared independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Act_of_Restoration_of_State_Independence_of_Georgia\&action=edit\&redlink=1) after a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_independence_referendum,_1991) held on 31 March.[^cite_note-GovtofGeorgia-111] Georgia was the first non-[Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states) republic of the Soviet Union to officially declare independence,[^cite_note-112] with Romania becoming the first country to recognize Georgia in August 1991.[^cite_note-113] On 26 May, Gamsakhurdia was elected president in the [first presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Georgian_presidential_election) with 86.5% of the vote on a turnout of over 83%.[^cite_note-114]&#x20;

Gamsakhurdia was soon deposed in a [bloody *coup d'état*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991%E2%80%9392_Georgian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat), from 22 December 1991 to 6 January 1992. The coup was instigated by part of the [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_of_Georgia) and a paramilitary organization called "[Mkhedrioni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mkhedrioni)" ("horsemen"). The country then became embroiled in a bitter [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_civil_war_of_1991%E2%80%931993), which lasted until December 1993. Simmering disputes within two regions of Georgia, [Abkhazia](18933375.md) and [South Ossetia](318381.md), between local separatists and the majority Georgian populations, erupted into widespread inter-ethnic violence and wars.[^cite_note-princeton-115] Supported by Russia, Abkhazia and South Ossetia achieved *de facto* independence from Georgia, with Georgia retaining control only in small areas of the disputed territories.[^cite_note-princeton-115] [Eduard Shevardnadze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduard_Shevardnadze) (Soviet [Minister of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Soviet_Union\)) from 1985 to 1991) was named as the head of Georgia's new government in March 1992[^cite_note-116] and was elected as head of state in [that year's elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Georgian_general_election), later as president in [1995](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Georgian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-117]&#x20;

During the [War in Abkhazia (1992–1993)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Abkhazia_\(1992%E2%80%931993\)), roughly 230,000 to 250,000 Georgians[^cite_note-hrwreport-118] were expelled from Abkhazia by Abkhaz separatists and North Caucasian militants (including Chechens). Around 23,000 Georgians fled South Ossetia.[^cite_note-1996_hrw-119]&#x20;

In 1994, Georgia was facing an economic crisis, with severe shortages of basics such as bread, water, electricity and heat.[^cite_note-120]&#x20;

### Rose revolution and UNM government

Main article: [Rose Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Revolution)



Protesters gathering at the [Tbilisi Assembly Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tbilisi_Assembly_Building) during the [Rose Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Revolution) of 2003

In 2003, Shevardnadze (who won re-election in 2000) was deposed by the [Rose Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Revolution), after Georgian opposition and international monitors asserted that the 2 November [2003 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Georgian_parliamentary_election) were marred by fraud.[^cite_note-121] The revolution was led by [Mikheil Saakashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Saakashvili), [Zurab Zhvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurab_Zhvania) and [Nino Burjanadze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nino_Burjanadze), former members and leaders of Shevardnadze's ruling party. Mikheil Saakashvili was elected as President of Georgia in 2004.[^cite_note-122]&#x20;

Following the Rose Revolution, a series of reforms were launched to strengthen the country's military and economic capabilities, as well as to reorient its foreign policy westwards. According to the US [Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_State), Georgia moved "from a near-[failed state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Failed_state) in 2003 to a relatively well-functioning market economy in 2014...\[and] Through dramatic police and institutional reforms, the government has eradicated low-level corruption".[^cite_note-123] The new government's efforts to reassert Georgian authority in the southwestern autonomous republic of [Adjara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjara) led to a major [crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajaria_Crisis) in 2004.[^cite_note-124]&#x20;

The country's newly pro-Western stance, along with accusations of Georgian involvement in the [Second Chechen War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Chechen_War),[^cite_note-125] resulted in a severe deterioration of [relations with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia-Russia_relations), fuelled also by Russia's open assistance and support to the two secessionist areas. Despite these increasingly difficult relations, in May 2005 Georgia and Russia reached a bilateral agreement[^cite_note-126] by which Russian military bases (dating back to the Soviet era) in [Batumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batumi) and [Akhalkalaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhalkalaki) were withdrawn. Russia withdrew all personnel and equipment from these sites by December 2007[^cite_note-127] while failing to withdraw from the [Gudauta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gudauta) base in Abkhazia, which it was required to vacate after the adoption of the [Adapted Conventional Armed Forces in Europe Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adapted_Conventional_Armed_Forces_in_Europe_Treaty) during the [1999 Istanbul summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Istanbul_summit).[^cite_note-128]&#x20;

### Russo-Georgian War

Main article: [Russo-Georgian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War)

See also: [International recognition of Abkhazia and South Ossetia](19042397.md)



A Russian tank during its invasion of Georgia.

Relations between Georgia and separatists in [Abkhazia](18933375.md) and [South Ossetia](318381.md) have been tense since the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1991. Relations between Russia and Georgia [severely deteriorated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_deportation_of_Georgians_from_Russia) into the April 2008 [Russo-Georgian diplomatic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Russo-Georgian_diplomatic_crisis).[^cite_note-shelling-129][^cite_note-tall_order-130] The [Russo-Georgian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War) of 2008 resulted in parts of Abkhazia and South Ossetia falling under the control of Russian-backed separatists, with Russian military bases being established in [those regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_in_Georgia).&#x20;

A bomb explosion on 1 August 2008 targeted a car transporting Georgian peacekeepers. South Ossetians were responsible for instigating this incident, which marked the opening of hostilities and injured five Georgian servicemen, then several South Ossetian militiamen were killed by snipers.[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-feud-132] South Ossetian separatists began shelling Georgian villages on 1 August. These artillery bombardments caused Georgian servicemen to return fire periodically.[^cite_note-shelling-129][^cite_note-feud-132][^cite_note-133][^cite_note-Allison-134][^cite_note-135]&#x20;

On 7 August 2008, the Georgian president Mikheil Saakashvili announced a unilateral [ceasefire](592591.md) and called for peace talks.[^cite_note-136] More attacks on Georgian villages (located in the South Ossetian conflict zone) were soon matched with gunfire from Georgian troops, who then proceeded to move in the direction of the capital of the [self-proclaimed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-proclaimed) Republic of South Ossetia ([Tskhinvali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tskhinvali)) on the night of 8 August, reaching its centre in the morning of 8 August.[^cite_note-Jamestown_Goals-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-139] According to Russian military expert [Pavel Felgenhauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavel_Felgenhauer), the Ossetian provocation was aimed at triggering Georgian retaliation, which was needed as a [pretext](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretext) for a Russian military invasion.[^cite_note-140] According to Georgian intelligence and several Russian media reports, parts of the regular (non-peacekeeping) Russian Army had already moved to South Ossetian territory through the [Roki Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roki_Tunnel) before the Georgian military action.[^cite_note-NYTFreshev-141][^cite_note-142]&#x20;

Russia accused Georgia of "aggression against South Ossetia" and began a large land, air and sea invasion of Georgia under the pretext of a "[peace enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_enforcement)" operation on 8 August 2008.[^cite_note-roudik-143][^cite_note-Allison-134] [Abkhaz forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_Abkhazia) opened a second front on 9 August with the [Battle of the Kodori Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Kodori_Valley), an attack on the Kodori Gorge, held by Georgia.[^cite_note-144] Tskhinvali was seized by the Russian military by 10 August.[^cite_note-radar-145] Russian forces occupied Georgian cities beyond the disputed territories.[^cite_note-new_front_deeper-146]&#x20;

During the conflict, there was a campaign of [ethnic cleansing against Georgians in South Ossetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing_of_Georgians_in_South_Ossetia), including destruction of Georgian settlements after the war had ended.[^cite_note-Fact-finding-147][^cite_note-Damage-148] The war displaced 192,000 people, though many were able to return to their homes after the war. One year later, around 30,000 ethnic Georgians remained displaced.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] In an interview published in *[Kommersant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kommersant)*, South Ossetian leader [Eduard Kokoity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduard_Kokoity) said he would not allow Georgians to return.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152]&#x20;

The [President of France](24899.md), [Nicolas Sarkozy](557007.md), negotiated a ceasefire agreement on 12 August 2008.[^cite_note-153] Russia recognized Abkhazia and South Ossetia as separate republics on 26 August.[^cite_note-kremlin-154] The Georgian government severed diplomatic relations with Russia.[^cite_note-break-155] Russian forces left the buffer areas bordering Abkhazia and South Ossetia on 8 October and the [European Union Monitoring Mission in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Monitoring_Mission_in_Georgia) was dispatched to the buffer areas.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] Since the war, Georgia has maintained that Abkhazia and South Ossetia are [occupied Georgian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_in_Georgia).[^cite_note-parliament.ge-158][^cite_note-159]&#x20;

### Georgian Dream government (2012–present)



[Bidzina Ivanishvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bidzina_Ivanishvili), founder and honorary chairman of [Georgian Dream](35807631.md).

In preparation for the [2012 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Georgian_parliamentary_election), Georgia implemented constitutional reforms to switch to a [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system), moving executive powers from the President to the Prime Minister. The transition was set to start with the 2012 parliamentary elections and to be completed with the [2013 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Georgian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-160]&#x20;

The [2012 Georgian parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Georgian_parliamentary_election) marked the defeat of the [United National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_National_Movement) and the victory of the [Georgian Dream](35807631.md) party—the first peaceful electoral transfer of power in Georgia.[^cite_note-161] The first year marked the contentious "cohabitation" period between the new Georgian Dream government and President Saakashvili, who retained his post until the [presidential election in October 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Georgian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] [Giorgi Margvelashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgi_Margvelashvili), nominated by Georgian Dream, won the election and succeeded Saakashvili, further solidifying the power of Georgian Dream.[^cite_note-165] [Bidzina Ivanishvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bidzina_Ivanishvili), a businessman who founded the Georgian Dream, served as its first Prime Minister before resigning in 2013. However, he continued to be the most influential person within the party, despite not holding any position until 2018.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] As for Saakashvili, he left Georgia shortly after the 2013 election and was convicted in 2018 in absentia on corruption charges and abuse of power, which he denied. Saakashvili continued to be the chairman of the United National Movement party from abroad, which now became the main opposition party[^cite_note-168]&#x20;

Georgian Dream won the [2016 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Georgian_parliamentary_election), achieving a constitutional [supermajority](638313.md) of 115 out of 150 seats.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] In the [2018 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Georgian_presidential_election), the Georgian Dream party backed independent [Salome Zurabishvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Zourabichvili), who won in the second round, becoming the first woman in Georgia to hold the office in full capacity.[^cite_note-172] This was the last direct election of a Georgian president, as additional constitutional reforms removed the popular vote.[^cite_note-Georgia_elects_Salome_Zurabishvili-173]&#x20;

In 2019, [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_protests_in_Georgia_\(country\)) erupted over the visit of Russian MP [Sergei Gavrilov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Gavrilov_\(politician\)) and his participation in the [Interparliamentary Assembly on Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interparliamentary_Assembly_on_Orthodoxy). The protesters demanded electoral reform to change the [mixed proportional-majoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-member_majoritarian_representation) voting system which they alleged greatly benefited the ruling party in the 2016 election. The ruling party conceded on some demands and condemned Gavrilov's visit. After international mediation to overcome the deep political crisis in the run-up to the [2020 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Georgian_parliamentary_election), an amended electoral system was adopted, specifically for those elections.[^cite_note-174] As result of the lowered threshold, nine parties were elected to parliament. Georgian Dream secured 48 percent of the votes, nearly the same as four years earlier, which translated into 90 out of 150 seats due to the more proportional electoral system with a highly reduced number of [majoritarian districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winner-take-all_system). However, the opposition made accusations of fraud, triggering another round of [political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_Georgian_political_crisis), lasting months. The opposition and protesting citizens demanded a new vote.[^cite_note-175] The political crisis was temporarily resolved by an EU brokered agreement in April 2021,[^cite_note-176] from which both the ruling Georgian Dream and the opposition United National Movement withdrew a few months later.[^cite_note-177]&#x20;

During the [Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_and_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), Georgia provided diplomatic and humanitarian support for Ukraine but did not join other countries in imposing sanctions on Russia. Since the beginning of the war, Georgia has topped the list of countries to which [Russian exiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_emigration_during_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) moved. Russians, just like many foreigners, were allowed to stay in Georgia for one year without a visa, though many Georgians began to view the presence of more Russian citizens in Georgia as a security risk.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] Shortly after the outbreak of the war, Georgia applied for membership of the EU at the same time as Ukraine and Moldova. While the latter two received the candidate status a few months later, Georgia was given conditions to receive the candidate status, which predominantly focused on judicial reforms, rule of law, de-oligarchization and addressing the highly polarized political and media climate.[^cite_note-180]&#x20;



Hundreds of protesters [obstructing traffic](53267635.md) at [Heroes Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heroes_Square)

In the following months, the relations with the [European Union](9317.md) worsened, and the Georgian Dream party turned from the pro-European social democratic one into the Eurosceptic and socially conservative party. In March 2023 Georgian Dream attempted to adopt the [Law on Transparency of Foreign Influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_funding_of_NGOs#Georgia), which requires [Non-Governmental Organizations](46539.md) (NGOs) to register as "agents of foreign influence" if they receive more than 20 per cent of their funding from abroad.[^cite_note-181] The discussions of the bill were determined amidst the protests and pressure from the [US State Department](31975.md), [United Nations](31769.md) and European Union.[^cite_note-182] A similar bill was introduced in April 2024 and adopted the next month.[^cite_note-183]&#x20;

In December 2023, despite not having fulfilled most conditions, Georgia was granted EU-candidate status. Instead, these criteria and additional ones, such as free and fair 2024 parliamentary elections, were made conditional for Georgia to move ahead to the actual accession talks.[^cite_note-184]&#x20;

The alleged irregularities and fraud in the 26 October [2024 Georgian parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Georgian_parliamentary_election) led to a [political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_political_crisis_\(2024%E2%80%93present\)) and [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Georgian_post-election_protests). The crisis was exacerbated after Prime Minister Irakli Kobakhidze announced the temporary suspension of the [accession process of Georgia's EU membership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_of_Georgia_to_the_European_Union) until 2028, citing blackmail.[^cite_note-185] Another round of political crisis developed amidst the inauguration of the ruling party candidate [Mikheil Kavelashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Kavelashvili) as the new president by the parliamentary electoral college, which the previous president [Salome Zourabichvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Zourabichvili), who went into opposition to the ruling party during the [2020–2021 Georgian political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_Georgian_political_crisis), described as illegitimate and refused to recognize the transfer of power.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Georgia_\(country\))



[Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) map of Georgia

Georgia is a mountainous country situated almost entirely in the [South Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Caucasus), while [some slivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Georgia_\(country\)) of the country are situated north of the [Caucasus Watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Caucasus#Watershed) in the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus).[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] The country lies between latitudes [41°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/41st_parallel_north) and [44° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/44th_parallel_north), and longitudes [40°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40th_meridian_east) and [47° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/47th_meridian_east), with an area of 67,900 km<sup>2</sup> (26,216 sq mi). The [Likhi Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likhi_Range) divides the country into eastern and western halves.[^cite_note-Cac-190] Historically, the western portion of Georgia was known as Colchis while the eastern plateau was called Iberia.[^cite_note-NAG-191]&#x20;

The [Greater Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Caucasus) Mountain Range forms the northern border of Georgia.[^cite_note-Cac-190] The main roads through the mountain range into Russian territory lead through the Roki Tunnel between [Shida Kartli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shida_Kartli) and North Ossetia and the [Darial Gorge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darial_Gorge) (in the Georgian region of [Khevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khevi)). The southern portion of the country is bounded by the [Lesser Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Caucasus) Mountains.[^cite_note-Cac-190] The Greater Caucasus Mountain Range is much higher in elevation than the Lesser Caucasus Mountains, with the highest peaks rising more than 5,000 metres (16,404 ft) [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_mean_sea_level).&#x20;



[Mount Kazbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Kazbek) in northeastern Georgia

The highest mountain in Georgia is Mount [Shkhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shkhara) at 5,203 metres (17,070 ft), and the second highest is Mount [Janga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janga_\(mountain\)) at 5,059 m (16,598 ft) above sea level. Other prominent peaks include [Mount Kazbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Kazbek) at 5,047 m (16,558 ft), [Shota Rustaveli Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shota_Rustaveli_Peak) 4,960 m (16,273 ft), [Tetnuldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetnuldi) 4,858 m (15,938 ft), [Ushba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ushba) 4,700 m (15,420 ft), and [Ailama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ailama) 4,547 m (14,918 ft).[^cite_note-Cac-190] Out of the abovementioned peaks, only Kazbek is of [volcanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic) origin. The region between [Kazbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazbek) and Shkhara (a distance of about 200 km (124 mi) along the Main Caucasus Range) is dominated by numerous glaciers.[^cite_note-NAG-191]&#x20;

The term Lesser Caucasus Mountains is often used to describe the mountainous (highland) areas of southern Georgia that are connected to the Greater Caucasus Mountain Range by the Likhi Range.[^cite_note-Cac-190] The overall region can be characterized as being made up of various, interconnected mountain ranges (largely of volcanic origin) and plateaus that do not exceed 3,400 metres (11,155 ft) in elevation. Prominent features of the area include the [Javakheti Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javakheti_Plateau), lakes, including [Tabatskuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabatskuri_Lake) and [Paravani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paravani_Lake), as well as mineral water and hot springs. Two major [rivers in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Georgia_\(country\)) are the [Rioni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rioni_River) and the [Mtkvari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mtkvari_River).[^cite_note-NAG-191]&#x20;

### Topography



Svaneti region of Georgia



Mountain lakes in the country's northwest

The landscape within the nation's boundaries is quite varied. Western Georgia's landscape ranges from low-land marsh-forests, swamps, and [temperate rainforests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_rainforest) to eternal snows and glaciers, while the eastern part of the country even contains a small segment of [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid) plains.[^cite_note-NAG-191]&#x20;

Much of the natural habitat in the low-lying areas of western Georgia has disappeared during the past 100 years because of [agricultural development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_development) and urbanization. A large majority of the forests that covered the Colchis plain are now virtually non-existent with the exception of the regions that are included in the national parks and reserves (e.g. [Lake Paliastomi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Paliastomi) area). At present, the forest cover generally remains outside of the low-lying areas and is mainly located along the foothills and the mountains. Western Georgia's forests consist mainly of deciduous trees below 600 metres (1,969 ft) [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_mean_sea_level) and contain species such as [oak](39696.md), [hornbeam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornbeam), [beech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Beech), [elm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elm), [ash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash_tree), and [chestnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_Chestnut). Evergreen species such as [box](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buxus) may also be found in many areas. About 1,000 of the 4,000 higher plants of Georgia are [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic).[^cite_note-192]&#x20;



The cave city of [Vardzia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vardzia), with the valley of the [Kura River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kura_\(Caspian_Sea\)) below

The west-central slopes of the [Meskheti Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meskheti_Range) in [Ajaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajaria) as well as several locations in Samegrelo and Abkhazia are covered by [temperate rain forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_rain_forests). Between 600–1,000 metres (1,969–3,281 ft) above sea level, the deciduous forest becomes mixed with both broad-leaf and coniferous species making up the plant life. The zone is made up mainly of beech, [spruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasian_Spruce), and [fir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordmann_Fir) forests. From 1,500–1,800 metres (4,921–5,906 ft), the forest becomes largely coniferous. The tree line generally ends at around 1,800 metres (5,906 ft) and the alpine zone takes over, which in most areas, extends up to an elevation of 3,000 metres (9,843 ft) [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_mean_sea_level).[^cite_note-NAG-191]&#x20;

The general landscape of eastern Georgia comprises numerous valleys and gorges that are separated by mountains. In contrast with western Georgia, nearly 85 per cent of the forests of the region are deciduous. Coniferous forests only dominate in the [Borjomi Gorge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borjomi_Gorge) and in the extreme western areas. Out of the deciduous species of trees, [beech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beech), [oak](39696.md), and [hornbeam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornbeam) dominate. Other deciduous species include several varieties of [maple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple), [aspen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen), ash, and [hazelnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazelnut).[^cite_note-NAG-191]&#x20;

At higher elevations above 1,000 metres (3,281 ft) [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_mean_sea_level) (particularly in the [Tusheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tusheti), [Khevsureti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khevsureti), and [Khevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khevi) regions), [pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_Pine) and [birch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birch) forests dominate. In general, the forests in eastern Georgia occur between 500–2,000 metres (1,640–6,562 ft) above sea level, with the alpine zone extending from 2,000–2,300 to 3,000–3,500 meters (6,562–7,546 to 9,843–11,483 ft). The only remaining large, low-land forests remain in the [Alazani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alazani) Valley of Kakheti.[^cite_note-NAG-191]&#x20;

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Georgia_\(country\))



Parts of Western Georgia have a subtropical climate, with frequent rain and plenty of green vegetation.



A small [alpine lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_lake) in northeastern Georgia

The climate of Georgia is extremely diverse, considering the nation's small size. There are two main climatic zones, roughly corresponding to the eastern and western parts of the country. The Greater Caucasus Mountain Range plays an important role in moderating Georgia's climate and protects the nation from the penetration of colder air masses from the north. The Lesser Caucasus Mountains partially protect the region from the influence of dry and hot air masses from the south.[^cite_note-Georgia-193]&#x20;

Much of western Georgia lies within the northern periphery of the humid subtropical zone with annual precipitation ranging from 1,000–2,500 mm (39–98 in), reaching a maximum during the Autumn months. The climate of the region varies significantly with elevation and while much of the lowland areas of western Georgia are relatively warm throughout the year, the foothills and mountainous areas (including both the Greater and Lesser Caucasus Mountains) experience cool, wet summers and snowy winters (snow cover often exceeds 2 metres or 6 feet 7 inches in many regions).[^cite_note-Georgia-193]&#x20;

Eastern Georgia has a transitional climate from humid subtropical to continental. The region's weather patterns are influenced both by dry Caspian air masses from the east and humid Black Sea air masses from the west. The penetration of humid air masses from the Black Sea is often blocked by mountain ranges ([Likhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likhi_Range) and [Meskheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meskheti)) that separate the eastern and western parts of the nation.[^cite_note-NAG-191] The wettest periods generally occur during spring and autumn, while winter and summer months tend to be the driest. Much of eastern Georgia experiences hot summers (especially in the low-lying areas) and relatively cold winters. As in the western parts of the nation, elevation plays an important role in eastern Georgia where climatic conditions above 1,500 metres (4,921 ft) are considerably colder than in the low-lying areas.[^cite_note-NAG-191]&#x20;

### Biodiversity

See also: [List of fauna of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_fauna_of_Georgia_\(country\)) and [List of fish of the Black Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_fish_of_the_Black_Sea)



[Georgian Shepherd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Shepherd) Dog



[East Caucasian tur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Caucasian_tur) on the cliffs of [Lagodekhi Protected Areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagodekhi_Protected_Areas)

Because of its high landscape diversity and low latitude, Georgia is home to about 5,601 species of animals, including 648 species of [vertebrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrate) (more than 1% of the species found worldwide) and many of these species are endemics.[^cite_note-194] A number of large [carnivores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivore) live in the forests, namely [brown bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear), [wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf), [lynxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynx) and [Caucasian leopards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panthera_pardus_tulliana). The [common pheasant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_pheasant) (also known as the Colchian pheasant) is an endemic bird of Georgia which has been widely introduced throughout the rest of the world as a [game bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_bird). The number of [invertebrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invertebrate) species is considered to be very high but data is distributed across a high number of publications.
