The spider checklist of Georgia, for example, includes 501 species.[^cite_note-Caucasus-spiders.info-195] The [Rioni River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rioni) may contain a breeding population of the critically endangered [bastard sturgeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bastard_sturgeon).[^cite_note-196]&#x20;

Slightly more than 6,500 species of [fungi](19178965.md), including lichen-forming species, have been recorded from Georgia,[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-cybertruffle1-198] but this number is far from complete. The true total number of fungal species occurring in Georgia, including species not yet recorded, is likely to be far higher, given the generally accepted estimate that only about seven per cent of all fungi worldwide have so far been discovered.[^cite_note-199] Although the amount of available information is still very small, a first effort has been made to estimate the number of fungal species endemic to Georgia, and 2,595 species have been tentatively identified as possible endemics of the country.[^cite_note-200] 1,729 species of plants have been recorded from Georgia in association with fungi.[^cite_note-cybertruffle1-198] According to the [International Union for Conservation of Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Union_for_Conservation_of_Nature), there are 4,300 species of vascular plants in Georgia.[^cite_note-201]&#x20;

Georgia is home to four ecoregions: [Caucasus mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_mixed_forests), [Euxine-Colchic deciduous forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euxine-Colchic_deciduous_forests), [Eastern Anatolian montane steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Anatolian_montane_steppe), and [Azerbaijan shrub desert and steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_shrub_desert_and_steppe).[^cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-202] It had a 2018 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 7.79/10, ranking it 31st globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-203]&#x20;

## Government and politics

Main article: [Politics of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Georgia_\(country\))



[Mikheil Kavelashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Kavelashvili)\
[President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Georgia)



[Irakli Kobakhidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irakli_Kobakhidze)\
[Prime Minister](563908.md)



[Orbeliani Palace](68206220.md) is the official seat of the [Georgian President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Georgia), an institution that has seen diminished power since 2018.

Georgia is a [representative democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_democracy) [parliamentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary) republic; the [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Georgia) serves as the largely ceremonial head of state,[^cite_note-Georgia_elects_Salome_Zurabishvili-173] whereas the Prime Minister is head of government. Executive power is vested in the [Cabinet of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Georgia), composed of ministers headed by the [Prime Minister](563908.md) and appointed by the Parliament. As of 2025, the presidency is [disputed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%932025_Georgian_constitutional_crisis) between [Salome Zourabichvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Zourabichvili), who claims to be the *de jure* head of state, and [Mikheil Kavelashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Kavelashvili), who was inaugurated by the ruling party following the widely disputed [2024 Georgian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Georgian_presidential_election). Since February 2024, the post of the Prime Minister has been occupied by [Irakli Kobakhidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irakli_Kobakhidze), whose legitimacy is also disputed.&#x20;

[Legislative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislature) authority is vested in the [Parliament of Georgia](3471146.md). It is unicameral and has 150 members, known as deputies, of whom 30 are elected by plurality to represent single-member districts, and 120 are chosen to represent parties by proportional representation. Members of parliament are elected for four-year terms.&#x20;

According to [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance)'s Global State of Democracy (GSoD) Indices and Democracy Tracker, Georgia performs in the mid-range on overall democratic measures, with particular strength in absence of corruption.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206]&#x20;

Different opinions exist regarding the degree of political freedom in Georgia. Saakashvili believed in 2008 that the country is "on the road to becoming a European democracy."[^cite_note-misha-207] In their 2022 report [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) lists Georgia as "partly free", recognizing a trajectory of democratic improvement surrounding the 2012–13 transfer of power but observed a process of democratic backslide in later years of the [Georgian Dream](35807631.md) rule.[^cite_note-208] In the 2023 [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist_Democracy_Index), the [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md) classifies Georgia as a "hybrid regime", which denotes an incomplete [democratic transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_transition) from [authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) to [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) characterized by elements of both systems.[^cite_note-209]&#x20;

In 2025, the annual [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices) of the [V-Dem Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Institute) classified Georgia for the first time as an [electoral autocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_autocracy), noting the country showed one of the biggest drops on the list and its largest one-year decline. V-Dem notes Georgia is considered to be in a so-called "bell-turn" episode or "failed democratization", meaning its autocratization took place less than five years after a period of democratization. The report describes that post-Saakashvili Georgia went through a period of democratization with improvements in judicial independence, freedom of speech, and civil liberties. The reversal of this trend started in 2018, with gradual deteriorations till 2023 when rapid democratic erosion set in, resulting in a plummeting of the score in 2024.[^cite_note-vdem2025-210]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Georgia_\(country\))

See also: [Russian-occupied territories in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_in_Georgia)



Map of Georgia highlighting the disputed territories of Abkhazia and Tskhinvali Region (*South Ossetia*), both of which are outside the control of the central government of Georgia

Georgia is [administratively divided](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Georgia_\(country\)) into 9 regions, 1 capital region, and 2 autonomous republics. These in turn are subdivided into 67 [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Georgia_\(country\)) and 5 self-governing cities.[^cite_note-211]&#x20;

Georgia contains two official autonomous regions, of which one has declared independence. Officially autonomous within Georgia,[^cite_note-212] the de facto independent region of Abkhazia declared independence in 1999.[^cite_note-213] In addition, another territory not officially autonomous has also declared independence. South Ossetia is officially known by Georgia as the Tskhinvali region, as it views "South Ossetia" as implying political bonds with Russian [North Ossetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Ossetia).[^cite_note-BBC_South_Ossetia-214] It was called [South Ossetian Autonomous Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Ossetian_Autonomous_Oblast) when Georgia was part of Soviet Union. Its autonomous status was revoked in 1990. De facto separate since Georgian independence, offers were made to give South Ossetia autonomy again, but in 2006 an unrecognized referendum in the area resulted in a vote for independence.[^cite_note-BBC_South_Ossetia-214]&#x20;

In both Abkhazia and South Ossetia large numbers of people had been given Russian passports, some through a process of forced [passportization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passportization) by Russian authorities.[^cite_note-215] This was used as a justification for Russian invasion of Georgia during the 2008 South Ossetia war after which Russia recognized the region's independence.[^cite_note-216] Georgia considers the regions as occupied by Russia.[^cite_note-parliament.ge-158][^cite_note-217] The two self-declared republics gained [limited international recognition](19042397.md) after the 2008 Russo-Georgian War. Most countries consider the regions to be Georgian territory [under Russian occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_in_Georgia).[^cite_note-smr-218]&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Georgia_\(country\))



President of Georgia [Salome Zourabichvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Zourabichvili), President of Moldova [Maia Sandu](40796149.md), President of Ukraine [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) and [President of the European Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_European_Council) [Charles Michel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Michel) during the 2021 [Batumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batumi) International Conference. In 2014, the EU signed [Association Agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Association_Agreement) with all three states.

The explicit western orientation of Georgia, deepening political ties with the US and European Union, notably through its EU and NATO membership aspirations, the US [Train and Equip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Train_and_Equip_Program) military assistance programme, and the construction of the [Baku–Tbilisi–Ceyhan pipeline](397567.md), increasingly strained Tbilisi's relations with Moscow in the early 2000s. Georgia's decision to boost its presence in the coalition forces in Iraq was an important initiative.[^cite_note-220] The European Union has identified Georgia as a prospective member,[^cite_note-EU_perspective-221] and Georgia has sought membership.[^cite_note-222]&#x20;

Georgia is currently working to become a full member of [NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization). In August 2004, the Individual Partnership Action Plan of Georgia was submitted officially to NATO. On 29 October 2004, the [North Atlantic Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Council) of NATO approved the [Individual Partnership Action Plan](21133.md#International_Partnership_Action_Plans) (IPAP) of Georgia, and Georgia moved on to the second stage of Euro-Atlantic Integration. In 2005, the agreement on the appointment of [Partnership for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partnership_for_Peace) (PfP) liaison officer between Georgia and NATO came into force, whereby a liaison officer for the South Caucasus was assigned to Georgia. On 2 March 2005, the agreement was signed on the provision of the host nation support to and transit of NATO forces and NATO personnel. On 6–9 March 2006, the IPAP implementation interim assessment team arrived in Tbilisi. On 13 April 2006, the discussion of the assessment report on implementation of the Individual Partnership Action Plan was held at NATO Headquarters, within 26+1 format.[^cite_note-223] In 2009 the Georgia-NATO Interparliamentary Council was created within the [NATO Parliamentary Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_Parliamentary_Assembly) to hold twice yearly meetings to discuss all aspects of Georgia-NATO cooperation. In 2017, a poll by the National Democratic Institute, an American NGO, revealed that the majority of Georgians and politicians in Georgia support the push for NATO membership.[^cite_note-224]&#x20;

In 2011, the [North Atlantic Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Council) designated Georgia as an "aspirant country".[^cite_note-225] Since 2014, Georgia–NATO relations are guided by the Substantial NATO–Georgia Package (SNGP), which includes the NATO–Georgia Joint Training and Evaluation Centre and facilitation of multi-national and regional military drills.[^cite_note-226]&#x20;



Georgians demonstrating in solidarity with Ukraine shortly after the 2022 Russian invasion.

In September 2019, Russian Foreign Minister [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md) said that "NATO approaching our borders is a threat to Russia."[^cite_note-227] He was quoted as saying that if [NATO](21133.md) accepts Georgian membership with the article on collective defence covering only Tbilisi-administered territory—i.e., excluding the Georgian territories Abkhazia and South Ossetia, both of which are currently Russian-supported [unrecognized breakaway republics—](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_with_limited_recognition)"we will not start a war, but such conduct will undermine our relations with NATO and with countries who are eager to enter the alliance."[^cite_note-LavrovNATO2019-228]&#x20;

[George W. Bush](3414021.md) became the first sitting U.S. president to visit the country.[^cite_note-229] The street leading to [Tbilisi International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tbilisi_International_Airport) has since been dubbed George W. Bush Avenue.[^cite_note-230] On 2 October 2006, Georgia and the European Union signed a joint statement on the agreed text of the Georgia–European Union Action Plan within the [European Neighbourhood Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Neighbourhood_Policy) (ENP). The Action Plan was formally approved at the EU–Georgia Cooperation Council session on 14 November 2006, in [Brussels](3708.md).[^cite_note-231] In June 2014, the EU and Georgia signed an [Association Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_Agreement), which entered into force on 1 July 2016.[^cite_note-232] On 13 December 2016, EU and Georgia reached the agreement on visa liberalization for Georgian citizens.[^cite_note-233] On 27 February 2017, the Council adopted a regulation on visa liberalization for Georgians travelling to the EU for a period of stay of 90 days in any 180-day period.[^cite_note-234]&#x20;

Georgia applied for EU membership on 3 March 2022, soon after the beginning of the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-ge-candidacy-235] In December 2023, Georgia was granted [EU Candidate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU_Candidate) status by the [European Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Council).[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] In November 2024, Prime Minister Irakli Kobakhidze declared the country's EU accession process would be paused until 2028, leading to protests.[^cite_note-240]&#x20;

### Military

Main articles: [Defense Forces of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Forces_of_Georgia), [Role of Georgia in the War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Role_of_Georgia_in_the_War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%932021\)), and [Conscription in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Georgia)



Georgian-built [Didgori Warrior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STC_Delta#Military_and_civilian_production)

Georgia's military is organized into [land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Land_Forces) and [air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Air_Force) forces collectively known as the Georgian Defense Forces (GDF); naval forces were merged into the Coast Guard in 2009, which falls under the Internal Affairs Minister. More than 20% of the GDF consists of [conscripts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Georgia).[^cite_note-\:1-241] The mission and functions of the GDF are based on the [Constitution of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Georgia_\(country\)), Georgia's Law on Defense and National Military Strategy, and international agreements to which Georgia is signatory. As of 2021, Georgia's military budget was 900[₾](904025.md) (\$280) million, of which newly two-thirds is allocated for maintaining defence forces readiness and potency development.[^cite_note-242] After its independence from the Soviet Union, Georgia began to develop its own [military industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_industry), namely through the state owned [STC Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STC_Delta).[^cite_note-243] The country produces a range of indigenous [military equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STC_Delta#Military_and_civilian_production), including armored vehicles, artillery systems, aviation systems, personal protection equipment, and small arms.[^cite_note-244]&#x20;

Georgian military personnel have served in several international operations. During later periods of the [Iraq War](5043324.md), Georgia had up to 2,000 soldiers serving in the American-led [Multi-National Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq).[^cite_note-245] Georgia also participated in the [NATO](21133.md)-led [International Security Assistance Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force) in [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%93present\)); with 1,560 troops in 2013, it was at that time the largest contributor among non-NATO countries[^cite_note-civil1-246] and in per capita terms.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248] Over 11,000 Georgian soldiers rotated through Afghanistan during the course of the war;[^cite_note-249] [32 were killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_casualties_in_Afghanistan),[^cite_note-250] mostly during the [Helmand campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmand_province_campaign), and 435 were wounded, including 35 amputees.[^cite_note-civil2-251][^cite_note-252]&#x20;

### Law enforcement

Main article: [Law enforcement in Georgia (country)](11222166.md)

In 2005, President [Mikheil Saakashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Saakashvili) fired the entire traffic police force (numbering around 30,000 police officers) of the Georgian National Police due to corruption.[^cite_note-mcclatchydc.com-253][^cite_note-254] A new force was then subsequently built around new recruits.[^cite_note-mcclatchydc.com-253] The [U.S. State Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_State_Department)'s Bureau of International Narcotics and Law-Enforcement Affairs has provided assistance to the training efforts and continues to act in an advisory capacity.[^cite_note-255]&#x20;

The new *Patruli* force was first introduced in the summer of 2005 to replace the traffic police, a force which was accused of widespread corruption.[^cite_note-256] The police introduced a 0–2–2 (currently, 1–1–2) [emergency dispatch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_telephone_number) service in 2004.[^cite_note-257]&#x20;

### Corruption

Main article: [Corruption in Georgia](41870283.md)

Prior to the [Rose Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Revolution), Georgia was among the most corrupt countries in the world.[^cite_note-forpol-258] However, following the reforms brought by the peaceful revolution, corruption in the country abated dramatically. In 2010, [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International) (TI) named Georgia "the best corruption-buster in the world."[^cite_note-econ-259] In 2012, the [World Bank](36752.md) called Georgia a "unique success" of the world in fighting corruption, noting "Georgia's experience shows that the vicious cycle of endemic corruption can be broken and, with appropriate and decisive reforms, can be turned into a virtuous cycle."[^cite_note-bank-260]&#x20;

Although Georgia has been very successful in reducing blatant forms of corruption, other, more subtle corrupt practices have been noted. For example, in its 2017 report, [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) observed that while most day-to-day corruption has been eliminated, there are some indications of a "[clientelistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clientelism) system" whereby the country's leadership may allocate resources in ways that generate the loyalty and support it needs to stay in power.[^cite_note-261] Since 2012 stagnation in corruption fighting efforts can be observed, according to Transparency International.[^cite_note-262] Since 2016 the Transparency International Corruption Perception Index hovers around 56 out of 100 points. In comparison, that places Georgia in the top 50 out of 180 countries, among Central European and [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean) EU member states.[^cite_note-263]&#x20;

### Human rights and freedom

Main article: [Human rights in Georgia](5404490.md)

Human rights in Georgia are guaranteed by the [country's constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Georgia_\(country\)). There is an independent human rights *public defender* elected by the [Parliament of Georgia](3471146.md) to ensure such rights are enforced.[^cite_note-264] Georgia has ratified the [Framework Convention for the Protection of National Minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Framework_Convention_for_the_Protection_of_National_Minorities) in 2005. NGO "Tolerance", in its alternative report about its implementation, speaks of a rapid decrease in the number of Azerbaijani schools and cases of appointing headmasters to Azerbaijani schools who do not speak the [Azerbaijani language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijani_language).[^cite_note-265]&#x20;

The government came under criticism for its alleged use of excessive force on 26 May 2011 when it dispersed [protesters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Georgian_protests) led by [Nino Burjanadze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nino_Burjanadze), among others, with tear gas and rubber bullets after they refused to clear [Rustaveli Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rustaveli_Avenue) for an independence day parade despite the expiration of their demonstration permit and despite being offered to choose an alternative venue.[^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268][^cite_note-ABC-AP-269] While human rights activists maintained that the protests were peaceful, the government pointed out that many protesters were masked and armed with heavy sticks and Molotov cocktails.[^cite_note-270] Georgian opposition leader Nino Burjanadze said the accusations of planning a coup were baseless, and that the protesters' actions were legitimate.[^cite_note-ABC-AP-269][^cite_note-271]&#x20;



Georgians protesting against [homophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homophobia) in front of parliament following anti-LGBT unrest

Since independence, Georgia maintained harsh policies against drugs, handing out lengthy sentences even for [marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marijuana) use. This came under criticism from human rights activists[^cite_note-272] and led to protests.[^cite_note-273] In response to lawsuits from civil society organizations, in 2018 the [Constitutional Court of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Georgia) ruled that "consumption of marijuana is an action protected by the right to free personality"[^cite_note-274] and that "\[Marijuana] can only harm the user's health, making that user him/herself responsible for the outcome. The responsibility for such actions does not cause dangerous consequences for the public."[^cite_note-275] With this ruling, Georgia became one of the [first countries in the world to legalize cannabis](168917.md), although using the drug in the presence of children is still illegal and punishable by fines or imprisonment.[^cite_note-276] Georgian prisons tend to be overcrowded with poor living conditions.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278]&#x20;

[LGBT individuals in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Georgia_\(country\)) frequently face harassment and violence.[^cite_note-279] Minor protections exist against discrimination on the basis of sexual orientation or gender identity. Since 2008 transgender people had been allowed to change their gender marker following [sex reassignment surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_reassignment_surgery). However, [a bill passed in 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_LGBT_propaganda_bill) seeks to remove many protections from LGBT people.[^cite_note-auto-280][^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282] The [European Union](9317.md), and various human rights organizations have condemned the legislation.[^cite_note-auto-280][^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] In 2024, Georgian President [Salome Zourabichvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Zourabichvili) condemned the high-profile murder of [Kesaria Abramidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kesaria_Abramidze), a transgender woman, and subsequently attended her funeral to pay respects.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] The law prohibits both surgeries (or other manipulations) for changing a gender and indication of another gender in state-issued ID documents, among other things.[^cite_note-287]&#x20;

## Economy

Main articles: [Economy of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Georgia_\(country\)) and [Agriculture in Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Georgia_\(country\))

| Rank | Trading partner | Share of total trade turnover |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | [European Union](9317.md) | 22.1% |
| 2 | [Turkey](11125639.md) | 12.2% |
| 3 | [United States](3434750.md) | 10.9% |
| 4 | [Russia](25391.md) | 10.1% |
| 5 | [China](5405.md) | 8.9% |



&#x20; Georgia

&#x20; Countries with whom Georgia signed Free Trade Agreement (FTA)

&#x20; Countries with whom Georgia has General Schemes of Preference (GSP)

Archaeological research demonstrates that Georgia has been involved in commerce with many lands and empires since ancient times, largely due its location on the Black Sea and later on the historical [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road). Gold, silver, copper and [iron](145352.md) have been mined in the [Caucasus Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains). [Georgian wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_wine) making is a very old tradition and a key branch of the country's economy. The country has sizeable [hydropower](14073.md) resources.[^cite_note-288] Throughout Georgia's modern history, [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Georgia_\(country\)) has played a significant economic role because of the country's climate and topography.&#x20;

For much of the 20th century, Georgia's economy was within the Soviet model of [command economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Command_economy). Since the fall of the USSR in 1991, Georgia embarked on a major structural reform designed to transition to a [free market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market) economy. As with all other [post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states), Georgia faced a severe economic collapse. The civil war and military conflicts in South Ossetia and Abkhazia aggravated the crisis. The agriculture and industry output diminished. By 1994 the gross domestic product had shrunk to a quarter of that of 1989.[^cite_note-289]&#x20;

Since the early 21st century visible positive developments have been observed in the economy of Georgia. In 2007, Georgia's [real GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_GDP) growth rate reached 12 per cent, making Georgia one of the fastest-[growing economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_growth) in Eastern Europe. Georgia has become more [integrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization) into the global trading network: its 2015 imports and exports account for 50% and 21% of GDP respectively.[^cite_note-wits15-290] Georgia's main imports are vehicles, ores, fossil fuels and pharmaceuticals. Main exports are ores, ferro-alloys, vehicles, wines, mineral waters and fertilizers.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] The World Bank dubbed Georgia "the number one economic reformer in the world" because it has in one year improved from rank 112th to 18th in terms of [ease of doing business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_Doing_Business_Index),[^cite_note-293] and by 2020 further improved its position to 6th in the world.[^cite_note-\:2-294] As of 2021, it ranked 12th in the world for [economic freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_of_Economic_Freedom). In 2019, Georgia ranked 61st on the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index) (HDI). Between 2000 and 2019, Georgia's HDI score improved by 17.7%.[^cite_note-undp_Briefing-295] Of factors contributing to HDI, education had the most positive influence[^cite_note-undp_Briefing-295] as Georgia [ranks in the top quintile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_Index) in terms of education.&#x20;

Georgia is developing into an international transport corridor through [Batumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batumi) and [Poti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poti) ports, [Baku–Tbilisi–Kars Railway line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baku-Tbilisi-Kars_Railway), an oil pipeline from [Baku](4566.md) through Tbilisi to [Ceyhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceyhan), the [Baku–Tbilisi–Ceyhan pipeline](397567.md) (BTC) and a parallel gas pipeline, the [South Caucasus Pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Caucasus_Pipeline).[^cite_note-296]&#x20;

Since coming to power the Saakashvili administration accomplished a series of reforms aimed at improving tax collection. Among other things a [flat income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_income_tax) was introduced in 2004.[^cite_note-297] As a result, budget revenues have increased fourfold and a once large [budget deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_deficit) has turned into a [surplus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_surplus).[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299]&#x20;

As of 2001, 54 per cent of the population lived below the national poverty line but by 2006 poverty decreased to 34 per cent and by 2015 to 10.1 per cent.[^cite_note-300] In 2015, the average monthly income of a household was 1,022.3[₾](904025.md) (about [\$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_dollar)426).[^cite_note-301] 2015 calculations place Georgia's [nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) at US\$13.98 billion.[^cite_note-302] Georgia's economy is becoming more devoted to [services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_economy) (as of 2016, representing 59.4 per cent of GDP), moving away from the [agricultural sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_sector) (6.1 per cent).[^cite_note-303] Since 2014, unemployment has been gradually decreasing each year but remained in double digits and worsened during the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md).[^cite_note-304] A perception of economic stagnation led to a 2019 survey of 1,500 residents finding unemployment was considered a significant problem by 73% of respondents, with 49% reporting their income had decreased over the prior year.[^cite_note-305]&#x20;

Georgia's telecommunications infrastructure is ranked the last among its bordering neighbours in the World Economic Forum's [Network Readiness Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_Readiness_Index) (NRI) – an indicator for determining the development level of a country's information and communication technologies. Georgia ranked number 58 overall in the 2016 NRI ranking,[^cite_note-NRI-306] up from 60 in 2015.[^cite_note-307] Georgia was ranked 56th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Georgia_\(country\))



[Gudauri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gudauri) is the most visited ski resort of Georgia.

Tourism is an increasingly significant part of the Georgian economy. In 2016, over 2.7 million tourists brought approximately US\$2.16 billion to the country.[^cite_note-310] In 2019, the number of international arrivals reached a record high of 9.3 million people.[^cite_note-311] with foreign exchange income in the year's first three-quarters amounting to over US\$3 billion. The country plans to host 11 million visitors by 2025 with annual revenues reaching US\$6.6 billion.[^cite_note-312] According to the government, there are 103 resorts in different [climatic zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) in Georgia. Tourist attractions include more than 2,000 [mineral springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_\(hydrology\)), over 12,000 historical and cultural monuments, four of which are recognized as [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Sites](44940.md) ([Bagrati Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrati_Cathedral) in [Kutaisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutaisi) and [Gelati Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gelati_Monastery), historical monuments of [Mtskheta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mtskheta), and [Upper Svaneti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Svaneti)).[^cite_note-313] Other tourist attractions are [Cave City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uplistsikhe), [Ananuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ananuri) Castle/Church, [Sighnaghi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sighnaghi) and [Mount Kazbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Kazbek). In 2018, more than 1.4 million tourists from Russia visited Georgia.[^cite_note-314]&#x20;

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Georgia_\(country\))



The [Georgian Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Railways) represent a vital artery linking the Black Sea and [Caspian Sea](19653787.md) – the shortest route between Europe and Central Asia.

Transport in Georgia is provided by [rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Railway), road, ferry, and air. The total length of [roads in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_in_Georgia_\(country\)), excluding the [occupied territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_in_Georgia), is 21,110 kilometres (13,120 mi) and railways – 1,576 km (979 mi).[^cite_note-315] Positioned in the Caucasus and on the coast of the Black Sea, Georgia is a key country for connecting the European Union with Asia while bypassing Russia, forming part of the [Trans-Caspian International Transport Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Caspian_International_Transport_Route).&#x20;

The Georgian railways represent an important transport artery for the Caucasus, as they make up the largest proportion of a route linking the Black and [Caspian Seas](19653787.md). In turn, this has allowed them to benefit in recent years from increased energy exports from neighbouring Azerbaijan to the European Union, Ukraine, and Turkey.[^cite_note-316] Passenger services are operated by the state-owned [Georgian Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Railway) while freight operations are carried out by a number of licensed operators. Since 2004, the Georgian Railways have been undergoing a rolling programme of fleet-renewal and managerial restructuring which is aimed at making the service provided more efficient and comfortable for passengers.[^cite_note-317] Infrastructural development has also been high on the agenda for the railways, with the key Tbilisi railway junction expected to undergo major reorganization in the near future.[^cite_note-318] Additional projects also include the construction of the economically important [Kars–Tbilisi–Baku railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kars%E2%80%93Tbilisi%E2%80%93Baku_railway), which was opened on 30 October 2017 and connects much of the Caucasus with Turkey by [standard gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_gauge) railway.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320]&#x20;



[Batumi Seaport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batumi_Seaport) in June 2020

Air and maritime transport is developing in Georgia, with the former mainly used by passengers and the latter for transport of freight. Georgia currently has four international airports, the largest of which is by far [Tbilisi International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tbilisi_International_Airport), hub for [Georgian Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Airways), which offers connections to many large European cities. Other airports in the country are largely underdeveloped or lack scheduled traffic, although, as of late, efforts have been made to solve both these problems.[^cite_note-321] There are a number of seaports along Georgia's Black Sea coast, the largest and most busy of which is the Port of Batumi; while the town is itself a seaside resort, the port is a major cargo terminal in the Caucasus and is often used by neighbouring Azerbaijan as a transit point for making energy deliveries to Europe. Scheduled and chartered passenger ferry services link Georgia with Bulgaria,[^cite_note-322] Romania, Turkey and Ukraine.[^cite_note-323]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Georgia_\(country\)) and [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgians)



Ethno-linguistic groups in the Caucasus region as of 1995

[^cite_note-ECMI-324]

Georgians do not fit into any of the main ethno-linguistic categories of Europe or Asia. The Georgian language, the most pervasive of the [Kartvelian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartvelian_languages), is not [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages), [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages), or [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages). The present-day Georgian or Kartvelian nation is thought to have resulted from the fusion of [indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples) inhabitants with various immigrants who moved into [South Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Caucasus) from [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) in remote antiquity.[^cite_note-325]&#x20;

Preliminary results of 2024 census indicates that the population is approximately 3,914,000 as of November 2024,[^cite_note-ReferenceA-13][^cite_note-a-326] an increase from 3,713,804 in the previous census in October 2014.[^cite_note-census2014summary-327][^cite_note-a-326] The population declined by 40,000 in 2021, a reversal of the trend towards stabilization of the last decade and, for the first time since independence, the population was recorded to be below 3.7 million. According to the 2014 census, [Ethnic Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_Georgians) form about 86.8 percent of the population, while the remainder includes ethnic groups such as [Abkhazians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abkhazians), [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_Georgia), [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people), [Azerbaijanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijanis_in_Georgia), [Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Greeks), [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Jews), [Kists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kists), [Ossetians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossetians), [Russians](59510.md), [Ukrainians](145144.md), [Yezidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yezidis) and others.[^cite_note-census2014summary-327][^cite_note-a-326] The Georgian Jews are one of the oldest Jewish communities in the world. According to the 1926 census there were 27,728 Jews in Georgia.[^cite_note-census1926-328][^cite_note-330] Georgia was also once home to significant ethnic German communities, numbering 11,394 according to the 1926 census.[^cite_note-census1926-328][^cite_note-332] Most of them [were deported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Germans#Bolnisi,_Georgia) during World War II.[^cite_note-333]&#x20;

The 2014 census, carried out in collaboration with the [United Nations Population Fund](63952.md) (UNFPA), found a population gap of approximately 700,000 compared to the 2014 data from the National Statistical Office of Georgia, Geostat, which was cumulatively built on the 2002 census. Consecutive research estimated the 2002 census to be inflated by 8 to 9 percent,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHakkert20171–4-334] which affected the annually updated population estimates in subsequent years. One explanation put forward by UNFPA is that families of emigrants continued to list them in 2002 as residents for fear of losing certain rights or benefits. Also, the population registration system from birth to death was non-functional. It was not until around 2010 that parts of the system became reliable again. With the support of the UNFPA, the demographic data for the period 1994–2014 has been retro-projected.[^cite_note-retroprojection-335] On the basis of that back-projection, Geostat has corrected its data for these years.&#x20;

The 1989 census recorded 341,000 ethnic [Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Georgia), or 6.3 percent of the population,[^cite_note-336] 52,000 [Ukrainians](145144.md) and 100,000 [Greeks in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Georgia).[^cite_note-emigration-337] The population of Georgia, including the breakaway regions, has declined by more than 1 million due to net emigration in the period 1990–2010.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHakkert201714-338][^cite_note-emigration-337] Other factors in the population decline include birth-death deficits for the period 1995–2010 and the exclusion of Abkhazia and South Ossetia from the statistics. Russia received by far the most migrants from Georgia. According to United Nations data, this totalled 625,000 by 2000, declining to 450,000 by 2019.[^cite_note-339] Initially, the out-migration was driven by non-Georgian ethnicities, but increasing numbers of Georgians emigrated as well,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones2013204-340] due to the war, the crisis-ridden 1990s, and the subsequent bad economic outlook. The 2010 Russian census recorded about 158,000 ethnic Georgians living in Russia,[^cite_note-341] with approximately 40,000 living in Moscow by 2014.[^cite_note-342] There were 184 thousand immigrants in Georgia in 2014 with most of them hailing from Russia (51.6%), Greece (8.3%), Ukraine (8.11%), Germany (4.3%), and Armenia (3.8%).[^cite_note-Immigration-343][^cite_note-a-326]&#x20;

In the early 1990s, following the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), violent [separatist](286527.md) conflicts broke out in the autonomous region of Abkhazia and [Tskhinvali Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Ossetian_Autonomous_Oblast). Many [Ossetians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossetians) living in Georgia left the country, mainly to Russia's [North Ossetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Ossetia).[^cite_note-1996_hrw-119] On the other hand, at least 160,000 Georgians left Abkhazia after the breakout of hostilities in 1993.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHakkert201743-344] Of the [Meskhetian Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meskhetian_Turk) who were [forcibly relocated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union) in 1944, only a tiny fraction returned to Georgia as of 2008.[^cite_note-345]&#x20;

In the 2024 Global Hunger Index, Georgia is one of 22 countries with a GHI score of less than 5. Differences between their scores are minimal. With a score under 5, Georgia has a level of hunger that is low.[^cite_note-346]&#x20;

### Languages

The most widespread language group is the [Kartvelian family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartvelian_languages), which includes Georgian, [Svan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svan_language), [Mingrelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mingrelian_language) and [Laz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laz_language).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoeder20023-348][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoeder20056-349][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGamkrelidze196669-350][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFähnrichSardzhveladze2000\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_June_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (June_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-351][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKajaia2001\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_June_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (June_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-352][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlimov199814-353] The official language of Georgia is [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language), with [Abkhaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abkhazian_language) having official status within the autonomous region of Abkhazia. Georgian is the primary language of 85.1 per cent of the population, followed by 6.8 per cent speaking [Azerbaijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijani_language), 3.5 per cent [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language), 1.4 per cent Russian, and 3.2 per cent other languages.[^cite_note-census2024-4][^cite_note-a-326]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Georgia_\(country\))

Main religions (2024)

[^cite_note-census2024-4]

[^cite_note-a-326]

1. [Orthodox Christian](10186.md) (82.0%)

2. [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) (11.1%)

3. [Armenian Apostolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church) (2.59%)

4. [Roman Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism_in_Georgia) (0.50%)

5. Others (3.76%)

Today, 82 percent of the population practices [Eastern Orthodox Christianity](10186.md), with the majority of these adhering to the national [Georgian Orthodox Church](433340.md).[^cite_note-census2024-4][^cite_note-a-326] The Georgian Orthodox Church is one of the world's oldest Christian churches, and claims apostolic foundation by [Saint Andrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Andrew).[^cite_note-354] In the first half of the 4th century, Christianity was adopted as the state religion of Iberia (present-day eastern Georgia), following the missionary work of [Saint Nino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Nino) of [Cappadocia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappadocia).[^cite_note-Toumanoff1963-355][^cite_note-Rapp2007-356] The Church gained [autocephaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocephaly) during the early Middle Ages; it was abolished during the Russian domination of the country, restored in 1917 and fully recognized by the [Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople) in 1989.[^cite_note-357]&#x20;

The special status of the Georgian Orthodox Church is officially recognized in the Constitution of Georgia and the [Concordat of 2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concordat_of_2002).[^cite_note-lorusso-358] While Georgia is officially a [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) country where religious institutions operate separately from the state, the Concordat established the Georgian Orthodox Church as the only recognized church in Georgia, which means other religious institutions have to seek recognition as civil organizations.[^cite_note-lorusso-358]&#x20;

Religious minorities of Georgia include Muslims (11.1 percent), [Armenian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church) (2.6 percent) and Roman Catholics (0.5 percent).[^cite_note-census2024-4][^cite_note-a-326] 0.6 percent of those recorded in the 2024 census declared themselves to be adherents of other religions, 2.7 percent refused or did not state their religion and 0.5 percent declared no religion at all.[^cite_note-census2024-4]&#x20;

[Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Georgia_\(country\)) is represented by both Azerbaijani [Shia](26961.md) Muslims (in the south-east), ethnic Georgian [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) Muslims in Adjara, Chechen sub-ethnic group of Sunni [Kists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kist_people) in the Pankisi Gorge, and [Laz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laz_language)-speaking Sunni Muslims as well as Sunni [Meskhetian Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meskhetian_Turks) along the border with Turkey. In Abkhazia, a minority of the Abkhaz population is also Sunni Muslim. There are also smaller communities of [Greek Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Muslims) (of [Pontic Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_Greek) origin) and [Armenian Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Muslims), both of whom are descended from Ottoman-era converts to Turkish Islam from [Eastern Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Anatolia) who settled in Georgia following the [Lala Mustafa Pasha's Caucasian campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lala_Mustafa_Pasha%27s_Caucasian_campaign) that led to the Ottoman conquest of the country in 1578. Georgian Jews trace the history of their community to the 6th century BC[^cite_note-359] but due to immigration to Israel, by the early 2000s their numbers had dwindled to several thousand.[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361]&#x20;

Despite the long history of religious harmony in Georgia,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpillingWong2008\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_June_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (June_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-362] there have been instances of religious discrimination and violence against "nontraditional faiths", such as [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses), by followers of the [defrocked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defrocked) Orthodox priest [Basil Mkalavishvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basil_Mkalavishvili).[^cite_note-363]&#x20;

In addition to traditional religious organizations, Georgia retains [secular and irreligious segments of society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_and_Irreligion_in_Georgia) (0.5 percent),[^cite_note-census2024-4] as well as a significant portion of religiously affiliated individuals who do not actively practice their faith.[^cite_note-364]&#x20;

### Education



[Elizabeth Orbeliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Orbeliani), Georgia's first woman professor and co-founder of [Tbilisi State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tbilisi_State_University) in 1918

The education system of Georgia has undergone sweeping, though controversial, modernization since 2004.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] [Education in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Georgia_\(country\)) is mandatory for all children aged 6–14.[^cite_note-tempus-367] The school system is divided into elementary (six years; ages 6–12), basic (three years; ages 12–15), and secondary (three years; ages 15–18), or alternatively vocational studies (two years). Access to higher education is given to students who have gained a secondary school certificate. Only those students who have passed the Unified National Examinations may enrol in a state-accredited higher education institution, based on ranking of the scores received at the exams.[^cite_note-368]&#x20;

Most of these institutions offer three levels of study: a bachelor's programme (three to four years); a master's programme (two years), and a doctoral programme (three years). There is also a certified specialist's programme that represents a single-level higher education programme lasting from three to six years.[^cite_note-tempus-367][^cite_note-369] As of 2016, 75 higher education institutions are accredited by the Ministry of Education and Science of Georgia.[^cite_note-370] [Gross primary enrolment ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_enrolment_ratio) was 117 percent for the period of 2012–2014, the 2nd highest in Europe after Sweden.[^cite_note-371]&#x20;

Tbilisi has become the main artery of the Georgian educational system, particularly since the creation of the [First Georgian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Georgia) in 1918 permitted the establishment of modern, Georgian-language educational institutions. Tbilisi is home to several major institutions of higher education in Georgia, notably the [Tbilisi State Medical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tbilisi_State_Medical_University), which was founded as Tbilisi Medical Institute in 1918, and the [Tbilisi State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tbilisi_State_University) (TSU), which was established in 1918 and remains the oldest university in the entire Caucasus region.[^cite_note-372] The number of faculty and staff (collaborators) at TSU is approximately 5,000, with over 35,000 students enrolled. The following four universities are also located in Tbilisi: [Georgian Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Technical_University),[^cite_note-373] which is Georgia's main and largest technical university, [The University of Georgia (Tbilisi)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_University_of_Georgia_\(Tbilisi\)),[^cite_note-374] as well as [Caucasus University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_University)[^cite_note-375] and [Free University of Tbilisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_University_of_Tbilisi).[^cite_note-376]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Georgia_\(country\))



[Sandro Akhmeteli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandro_Akhmeteli), Georgia's preeminent theater director and a critic of the [Great Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Terror).

Georgian culture evolved over thousands of years from its foundations in the [Iberian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Iberia_\(antiquity\)) and Colchian civilizations.[^cite_note-377] Georgian culture enjoyed a [renaissance and golden age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_Golden_Age) of [classical literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_literature), arts, philosophy, architecture and science in the 11th century.[^cite_note-378] Georgian culture was influenced by [Classical Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece), the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), the various [Iranian empires](64213.md#Classical_antiquity) (notably the [Achaemenid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire), [Parthian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire), [Sassanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sassanian_Empire), [Safavid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Empire), and [Qajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_Empire) empires),[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] and later, since the 19th century, by the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-Jones2003-383]&#x20;

This long history has provided a national narrative which encompasses the successful preservation of unique culture and identity in a consistent territory, despite external pressures. Christianity and the Georgian language are particularly important national identifiers.[^cite_note-Batiashvili2012-384] These cultural, religious, and later political attributes are associated with a European and Western identity, based on a national perception of these attributes that contrasts with surrounding powers.[^cite_note-Jones2003-383][^cite_note-Batiashvili2012-384][^cite_note-385][^cite_note-carnegie21-386] This self-identity is stronger among the dominant ethnic Georgian population than in the country's minority groups.[^cite_note-carnegie21-386]&#x20;

Georgia is known for its [folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore), traditional music, dances, theatre, cinema, and art. Notable painters from the 20th century include [Niko Pirosmani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niko_Pirosmani), [Lado Gudiashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lado_Gudiashvili), [Elene Akhvlediani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elene_Akhvlediani); notable ballet choreographers include [George Balanchine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Balanchine), [Vakhtang Chabukiani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vakhtang_Chabukiani), and [Nino Ananiashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nino_Ananiashvili); notable poets include [Galaktion Tabidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaktion_Tabidze), [Lado Asatiani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lado_Asatiani), and [Mukhran Machavariani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhran_Machavariani); and notable theatre and film directors include [Robert Sturua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Sturua), [Tengiz Abuladze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengiz_Abuladze), [Giorgi Danelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgi_Daneliya), and [Otar Ioseliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otar_Ioseliani).[^cite_note-Lang_David,_Georgians-387]&#x20;

### Architecture and arts



[Khertvisi Fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khertvisi_Fortress), an example of [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval) Georgian architecture

[Georgian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Georgia_\(country\)) has been influenced by many civilizations. There are several [architectural styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_styles) for [castles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castles), [towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower), [fortifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortifications) and churches. The [Upper Svaneti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Svaneti) fortifications, and the castle town of [Shatili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shatili) in [Khevsureti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khevsureti), are some of the finest examples of medieval Georgian [castle architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_architecture). Other architectural features of Georgia include [Rustaveli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rustavelis_Gamziri) Avenue in Tbilisi and the [Old Town District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Tbilisi).&#x20;

Georgian [ecclesiastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastic) art is one of the most notable aspects of Georgian [Christian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_architecture), which combines the classical [dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome) style with the original [basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica) style, forming what is known as the Georgian cross-dome style. Cross-dome architecture developed in Georgia during the 9th century; before that, most Georgian churches were basilicas. Other examples of Georgian ecclesiastic architecture can be found outside Georgia: [Bachkovo Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachkovo_Monastery) in Bulgaria (built in 1083 by the Georgian military commander Grigorii Bakuriani), [Iviron monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iviron_monastery) in Greece (built by Georgians in the 10th century), and the [Monastery of the Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastery_of_the_Cross) in Jerusalem (built by Georgians in the 9th century). One of the most famous late 19th/early 20th century Georgian artists was [primitivist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primitivist) painter [Niko Pirosmani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niko_Pirosmani).[^cite_note-388]&#x20;

### Literature

The [Georgian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language), and the Classical Georgian literature of the poet [Shota Rustaveli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shota_Rustaveli), were revived in the 19th century after a long period of turmoil, laying the foundations of the [romantics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) and novelists of the modern era such as [Grigol Orbeliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigol_Orbeliani), [Nikoloz Baratashvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikoloz_Baratashvili), [Ilia Chavchavadze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilia_Chavchavadze), [Akaki Tsereteli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akaki_Tsereteli), and [Vazha-Pshavela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vazha-Pshavela).[^cite_note-Lang_David,_Georgians-387] The Georgian language is written in three unique [scripts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_scripts) which, according to traditional accounts, were invented by King [Pharnavaz I of Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharnavaz_I_of_Iberia) in the 3rd century BC.[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390]&#x20;

### Media

Main article: [Mass media in Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Georgia_\(country\))

Television, magazines, and newspapers in Georgia are all operated by both state-owned and for-profit corporations which depend on advertising, [subscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subscription), and other sales-related revenues. The [Constitution of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Georgia_\(country\)) guarantees freedom of speech. The media environment of Georgia remains the freest and most diverse in the [South Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Caucasus),[^cite_note-391] despite the long-term politicization and polarization affecting the sector. The political struggle for control over the public broadcaster has left it without a direction in 2014 too.[^cite_note-FH-392]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Georgia_\(country\))

Georgia has an ancient musical tradition, which is primarily known for its early development of [polyphony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyphony). Georgian polyphony is based on three vocal parts, a unique tuning system based on perfect fifths, and a harmonic structure rich in parallel fifths and dissonances. Three types of polyphony have developed in Georgia: a complex version in Svaneti, a dialogue over a bass background in the Kakheti region, and a three-part partially improvised version in western Georgia.[^cite_note-393] The Georgian folk song "[Chakrulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakrulo)" was one of 27 musical compositions included on the [Voyager Golden Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voyager_Golden_Record) that were sent into space on [Voyager 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voyager_2) on 20 August 1977.[^cite_note-394]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Georgian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_cuisine)



Rather than serving food in [courses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Course_\(food\)), traditional *supras* often present all that a host has to offer.

[Georgian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_cuisine) and [wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_wine) have evolved through the centuries, adapting traditions in each era. One of the most unusual traditions of dining is [supra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supra_\(feast\)), or *Georgian table*, which is also a way of socializing with friends and family. The head of *supra* is known as [tamada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamada). He also conducts the highly philosophical toasts, and makes sure that everyone is enjoying themselves. Various historical regions of Georgia are known for their particular dishes: for example, [khinkali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khinkali) (meat dumplings), from eastern mountainous Georgia, and [khachapuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khachapuri), mainly from [Imereti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imereti), [Samegrelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samegrelo) and Adjara.[^cite_note-Tuskadze2017-395]&#x20;

### Wine

Main article: [Georgian wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_wine)



A glass of [Mukuzani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukuzani). Wine-making is a traditional component of the Georgian economy.

Georgia is [one of the oldest wine-producing countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_wine) in the world. Archaeology indicates that fertile valleys and slopes in and around Georgia have been home to [grapevine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grapevine) cultivation and [neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) wine production ([Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language): ღვინო, *ɣvino*) for millennia.[^cite_note-Vinologue-396][^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpillingWong2008128-399] Local traditions associated with wine are entwined with its national identity.[^cite_note-Vinologue-396] In 2013, [UNESCO](21786641.md) added the ancient traditional Georgian winemaking method using the [Kvevri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kvevri) clay jars to the UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage Lists.[^cite_note-Vinologue-396][^cite_note-ich.unesco.org-400]&#x20;

Georgia's moderate climate and moist air, influenced by the [Black Sea](3386.md), provide the best conditions for vine cultivation. The soil in vineyards is so intensively cultivated that the grapevines grow up the trunks of fruit trees, eventually hanging down along the fruit when they ripen. This method of cultivation is called *maglari*.[^cite_note-401] Among the best-known Georgian wine regions are [Kakheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakheti) (further divided into the micro-regions of [Telavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telavi) and [Kvareli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kvareli)), [Kartli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartli), [Imereti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imereti), [Racha-Lechkhumi and Kvemo Svaneti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racha-Lechkhumi_and_Kvemo_Svaneti), [Adjara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjara) and Abkhazia.&#x20;

Georgian wine has been a contentious issue in recent relationships with Russia. [Political tensions with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia%E2%80%93Russia_relations) have contributed to the 2006 [Russian embargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Russian_ban_of_Moldovan_and_Georgian_wines) of Georgian wine, Russia claimed Georgia produced [counterfeit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterfeit) wine. It was an "official" reason, but the instability of economic relations with Russia is well known, as they use the economic ties for political purposes.[^cite_note-rferl_1076202-402] Counterfeiting problems stem from mislabelling by foreign producers and falsified "Georgian Wine" labels on wines produced outside of Georgia and imported into Russia under the auspices of being Georgian produced.[^cite_note-rferl_1076202-402] The shipment of counterfeit wine has been primarily channelled through [Russian](9787373.md) managed customs checkpoints in Russian-occupied Georgian territories Abkhazia and South Ossetia, where no inspection and regulation occurs.[^cite_note-rferl_1076202-402]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Georgia (country)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Georgia_\(country\))



[Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_national_rugby_union_team) playing [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italy_national_rugby_union_team) at rugby in the [Adjarabet Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjarabet_Arena) in Batumi

The most popular sports in Georgia are [football](10568.md), basketball, [rugby union](25405.md), [wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrestling), judo, and [weightlifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting). Rugby is considered Georgia's national sport.[^cite_note-403] Historically, Georgia has been famous for its physical education; the [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) were fascinated with Georgians' physical qualities after seeing the training techniques of ancient [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Iberia_\(antiquity\)).[^cite_note-404] Wrestling remains a historically important sport of Georgia, and some historians think that the [Greco-Roman style of wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_wrestling) incorporates many Georgian elements.[^cite_note-405]&#x20;

Within Georgia, one of the most popularized styles of wrestling is the Kakhetian style. There were a number of other styles in the past that are not as widely used today. For example, the [Khevsureti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khevsureti) region of Georgia has three styles of wrestling. Other popular sports in 19th century Georgia were [polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polo), and [Lelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lelo_burti), a traditional Georgian game very similar to [rugby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_football).[^cite_note-Completerugby-406]&#x20;

The first and only race circuit in the Caucasian region is located in Georgia. [Rustavi International Motorpark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rustavi_International_Motorpark) originally built in 1978, was re-opened in 2012 after total reconstruction[^cite_note-407] costing \$20 million. The track satisfies the [FIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIA) Grade 2 requirements and currently hosts the [Legends car racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legends_car_racing) series and Formula Alfa competitions.[^cite_note-408]&#x20;

Basketball was always one of the notable sports in Georgia, and Georgia had a few very famous [Soviet Union national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union_national_basketball_team) members, such as [Otar Korkia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otar_Korkia), [Mikheil Korkia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikheil_Korkia), [Zurab Sakandelidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurab_Sakandelidze) and [Levan Moseshvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levan_Moseshvili). [Dinamo Tbilisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BC_Dinamo_Tbilisi) won the prestigious [EuroLeague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroLeague) competition in [1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961%E2%80%9362_FIBA_European_Champions_Cup). Georgia has had five players in the [NBA](22093.md): [Vladimir Stepania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Stepania), [Jake Tsakalidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Tsakalidis), [Nikoloz Tskitishvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikoloz_Tskitishvili), [Tornike Shengelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornike_Shengelia) and former [Golden State Warriors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_State_Warriors) centre [Zaza Pachulia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaza_Pachulia). Other notable basketball players include the two time EuroLeague champion [Giorgi Shermadini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgi_Shermadini) and EuroLeague players [Manuchar Markoishvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuchar_Markoishvili) and [Viktor Sanikidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Sanikidze). The sport is regaining its popularity in the country recently, and the [Georgia national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_national_basketball_team) has qualified for the [EuroBasket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket) tournament five consecutive times since its first appearance in 2011.&#x20;

There are a number of world-class Georgian MMA fighters. [Ilia Topuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilia_Topuria), [Merab Dvalishvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merab_Dvalishvili), [Giga Chikadze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giga_Chikadze) and [Roman Dolidze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Dolidze) are highly ranked fighters currently signed with the [UFC](169660.md).&#x20;

Georgian athletes have won a total of 40 Olympic medals, mostly in wrestling, judo and weightlifting.[^cite_note-409] Competitive bodybuilding sanctioned by the European [IFBB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_of_BodyBuilding_and_Fitness) is also popular in the country.[^cite_note-410]&#x20;

[^cite_note-misha-207]: &#x20;Berry, Lynn (7 January 2008). ["Georgia Leader: Country on Right Track"](https://web.archive.org/web/20080108095512/http://www.foxnews.com/wires/2008Jan07/0%2C4670%2CGeorgiaPresidentialElection%2C00.html). Fox News. Archived from [the original](http://www.foxnews.com/wires/2008Jan07/0,4670,GeorgiaPresidentialElection,00.html) on 8 January 2008. Retrieved 17 October 2008. In an interview with The Associated Press, Saakashvili acknowledged Georgia's path had not been smooth but said the election demonstrated that the former Soviet republic was on the road to becoming a European democracy.

[^cite_note-208]: &#x20;["Georgia: Freedom in the World 2022 Country Report"](https://freedomhouse.org/country/georgia/freedom-world/2022). [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230205015608/https://freedomhouse.org/country/georgia/freedom-world/2022) from the original on 5 February 2023. Retrieved 5 February 2023. Georgia holds regular and competitive elections. Its democratic trajectory showed signs of improvement during the period surrounding a change in government in 2012–13, but recent years have featured backsliding. Oligarchic influence affects the country's political affairs, policy decisions, and media environment, and the rule of law is undermined by politicization. Civil liberties are inconsistently protected.
